Universal extension spaces and modular maps: unveiling irreducible components of Brill-Noether loci of stable bundles on a general ν \nu -gonal curve
Youngook Choi, Flaminio Flamini, Seonja Kim

Abstract
. We investigate the Brill–Noether theory of rank-two, degree- d d , stable vector bundles of speciality 3 3 on a general ν \nu -gonal curve C C of genus g g . Our approach leverages universal extension spaces, modular maps, and recent advancements in rank-one Brill-Noether theory over Hurwitz spaces. We establish existence criteria for the corresponding Brill-Noether loci and provide a comprehensive description of their irreducible components. We moreover prove that these components exhibit diverse geometric behaviors, categorized by their regularity, superabundance, and the properties of their general points. Notably, for specific degrees d d , we prove the coexistence of multiple superabundant components alongside a regular one. Using specialization techniques, we uncover a stratification into locally closed subschemes within these components and provide insights into their birational geometry and local structure. Furthermore, our results yield also consequences for Brill-Noether loci of stable, rank-two bundles with a fixed general determinant.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14H60, 14H10, 14D20, 14D22, 14J26, 14E05, 14B10, 14N05

Introduction

Brill-Noether theory on smooth, complex projective curves
C
of genus
g
explores the geometry of maps
C\to\mathbb{P}^{r}
of degree
d
. These maps correspond to the Brill-Noether loci:

W_{d}^{r}(C):=\left\{L\in\text{Pic}^{d}(C)\mid h^{0}(C,L)\geq r+1\right\}\subseteq\text{Pic}^{d}(C).

For a
general curve

C
(i.e. when
C
corresponds to a general point of the
moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{g}
of curves of genus
g
) the properties of
W_{d}^{r}(C)
— such as non-emptiness, dimension, and smoothness — are well-established by main theorems from the 1970-80’s (due to several authors as D. Eisenbud-J. Harris,  W. Fulton-R. Lazarsfeld, D. Gieseker, P. Griffiths-J. Harris, G. Kempf, S. Kleiman-D. Laksov, K. Petri, just to mention a few) which give good descriptions of the geometric properties of such
W^{r}_{d}(C)
’s, which turn out to be determined by the invariants
(g,r,d)
, cf. e.g.
[
2
]
.

In contrast, the theory for higher-rank vector bundles remains partially open, even for
C
a general curve. Precisley, let
U_{C}(n,d)
be the moduli space of
(semi)stable vector bundles
of rank
n
and degree
d
, and
U^{s}_{C}(n,d)
be the open dense subset of
stable bundles
. For any non-negative integer
k
, one can define the
Brill-Noether
locus via (locally) determinantal subschemes as:

B^{k}_{n,d}:=\left\{[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(n,d)\mid h^{0}(\mathcal{F})\geq k\right\}\subseteq U_{C}(n,d).

Focusing on the rank
n=2
case, we simply denote
U_{C}(d):=U_{C}(2,d)
and
B^{k}_{d}:=B^{k}_{2,d}
. These loci are central to e.g. applications in birational geometry
[
5
,
26
]
, Hilbert schemes of curves and surfaces
[
9
,
11
]
and are also related to Bridgeland stability
[
16
]
.

Using Serre duality and stability considerations, one may focus in the range
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
with
k=k_{i}:=d-2g+2+i
, for any integer
i\geq 1
which is called the
speciality
of the vector bundles.
The expected dimension of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is the so called
rank-two Brill-Noether number
:

\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}:=4g-3-ik_{i}.

An irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
is said to be
regular
if it is generically smooth of dimension
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
, and
superabundant
otherwise. Unlike the line bundle case,
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
may exhibit unexpected behavior even for
C
a general curve, i.e. with
general moduli
(cf. e.g.
[
7
,
8
,
17
]
and also Remark
1.7
-(b) below).

Nevertheless, for
i=1
notably
B^{k_{1}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
is known to be irreducible and of the expected dimension for any smooth
C
of genus
g
, cf.
[
24
,
33
]
.

For speciality
i=2
, N. Sundaram
[
33
]
proved that
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
is not empty for any smooth, irreducible projective curve
C
and for odd degree
2g-1\leq d\leq 3g-4
; later on M. Teixidor I Bigas generalized Sundaram’s result as follows:

Result
(
[
34
,
35
]
)
For any
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
and for any smooth, irreducible, projective curve
C
of genus
g
the locus
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is either empty or it contains a component
\mathcal{B}
of (expected) dimension
\rho^{k_{2}}_{d}=8g-2d-11
.

When in particular
C
is assumed to be a general curve, i.e.
C
with general moduli, then such a
\mathcal{B}
is the only component of
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, hence
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is irreducible, moreover it is regular.

It is clear that, when
C
of genus
g
is assumed to be with
general moduli
, one can be supported by appropriate (and very delicate) degeneration arguments together with the theory of
limit linear series
in the sense of D. Eisenbud and J. Harris (cf.
[
15
]
). This is exactly the approach that e.g. M. Teixidor I Bigas used to get theorem below for
B^{k_{i}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
,
i\geq 2
, letting
C
degenerate to a chain of
g
elliptic curves.

Result
(
[
36
]
)
If
i\geq 2
and
d-2g+2+i\geq 2
, then
B^{k_{i}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
is non-empty on
C
a general curve of genus
g
, i.e.
C
with general moduli, for
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}\geq 1
and
d
odd or for
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}\geq 5
and
d
even. In all these cases there is one component of
B^{k_{i}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
of the expected dimension.

In the present paper, we study rank-two Brill-Noether theory on smooth curves
C
of genus
g
equipped with some
“atypical”
maps
f
which force
C
to be not general in moduli (i.e. curves with
special moduli
). The basic and most natural case occurs when

f:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\mbox{deg}(f)=\nu
with
\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
(the integer
\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
being the
general gonality
, i.e. the gonality of a curve of genus
g
with general moduli), and when
C
is
general
among genus-
g
curves possessing such a degree-
\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
map
f
onto
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(the case
\nu=2
, resp.
3
, being the classical case of
hyperelliptic
curves studied by Clifford
[
13
]
in 1878, resp. of
trigonal
curves studied by Maroni
[
25
]
in 1946). The term “general” in this context can be made more precise by considering the
Hurwitz scheme

\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}
which is irreducible, parametrizing branched covers
C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
\nu
and genus
g
, and which admits a natural
modular map

\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{M}_{g},

given by forgetting maps
f
. When
\nu\geq\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
, the map
\Phi
is dominant and
C
is with general moduli, as above. This is the reason why we restrict our attention to cases
\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
and refer to
C
to be a
general
\nu
-gonal
curve of genus
g
when it corresponds to a general point
[C]
of
{\rm Im}(\Phi)
.

In the forthcoming paper
[
10
]
we prove:

Result
(
[
10
]
)
For any
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
and on a general
\nu
-gonal curve
C
of genus
g
, according to the choice of
d
, the Brill-Noether locus
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is

\bullet
either empty, or

\bullet
it consists of only one irreducible component
B_{\rm reg,2}
(which coincides with the component
\mathcal{B}
mentioned in Teixidor’s result above), which is also regular and uniruled, providing also an explicit description of the stable vector bundle corresponding to the general point of
B_{\rm reg,2}
, or

\bullet
it consists of two irreducible component,
B_{\rm reg,2}
as above and also
another component
B_{\rm sup,2}
which is superabundant, being of dimension
6g-d-2\nu-6>\rho^{k_{2}}_{d}=8g-2d-11
, generically smooth and ruled, providing also an explicit description of the stable vector bundle corresponding to the general point of this extra component.

The present paper focuses instead on speciality
i=3
. We use some recent results in Brill-Noether theory of line bundles on a general
\nu
-gonal curve
C
as in
[
14
,
16
,
19
,
22
,
23
,
27
]
in order to study and classify irreducible components of Brill-Noether loci
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
when
C
is a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
, with
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
.

Our methodology focuses on obtaining explicit geometric descriptions of rank-two stable bundles
\mathcal{F}
representing general points of the irreducible components of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. These bundles are characterized by their presentations as extensions of line bundles:

(
*
)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to L\to 0,

where
L
and
N
are chosen from rank-one Brill-Noether loci on the
\nu
-gonal curve
C
. This approach, rooted in the work of Segre
[
30
]
and modernized by e.g. Beauville
[
5
]
, Bertram–Feinberg
[
6
,
7
]
and Mukai
[
26
]
, allows us to translate rank-two theory into the study of special unisecants on the ruled surface
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
.

We construct irreducible universal extension spaces
\mathcal{W}
parametrizing triples
(N,L,\mathcal{F})
; the bundle
\mathcal{F}
is defined in such a way that its speciality, which is obviously related to specialities of
L
and
N
and to the coboundary map
\partial:H^{0}(C,L)\to H^{1}(C,N)
, is preserved to
h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})=3
. Once the general bundle
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{W}
is proved to be also stable, we can define a rational modular map:

\pi:\mathcal{W}\dasharrow U^{s}_{C}(d),\quad(N,L,\mathcal{F})\mapsto[\mathcal{F}].

Then one has to show that image of
\pi
actually constitutes an irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. This framework enables us to categorize such components as
first
,
second
, or
modified type
based on the properties of the presentation
(*)
at a general point
[\mathcal{F}]
of such a component. The parametric nature of
\mathcal{W}
provides explicit strategies to study also the birational geometry of
\mathcal{B}
and to establish degree-based bounds for existence; for instance,
regular components
cannot exist for
d\geq\frac{10}{3}g-5
(i.e.
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}<0
). More precisely, the main result of this paper is the following:

Main Theorem
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
and let
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
be any integer. Then, for
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, one has the following cases:

(A) For
g\geq 4
,
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
and
4g-6\leq d\leq 4g-4
, one has
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)=\emptyset
.

(B) For
g\geq 15
,
3\leq\nu<\frac{g-2}{4}
and
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
, one has
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)=\emptyset
.

(C) Concerning irreducible components of first type (in the sense of Definition
3.3
below), we have the following situation:

(C-i) If
g\geq 4
,
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
and either
2g-2\leq d\leq 2g-7+2\nu
or
4g-4-4\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
, there is no irreducible component of first type of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
.

(C-ii) If
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}(B_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)})=6g-6-4\nu-d.

In particular
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(1-a)}
, when
d\geq 2g-5+2\nu
whereas it is regular, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,\,3,\,(1-a)}
, when otherwise
d=2g-6+2\nu
.

Furthermore, its general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in B_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
, speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is of first type (as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
3.2
below), fitting in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
, whereas
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
is general. Moreover
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
is the only irreducible component whose general point arises from a bundle
\mathcal{F}
of first type (in the sense of Definition
3.3
below) on
C
.

(D) Concerning irreducible components of modified type (in the sense of Definition
3.3
below), we have the following situation:

(D-i) If either
g\geq 6
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{2}
,
3g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
and
\nu+1\leq s\leq g-\nu+1
or
g\geq 8
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+4}{4}
,
3g-3-\nu\leq d\leq 3g-6
and
g-\nu+2\leq s\leq g-1
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and superabundant, being of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is obtained by natural modification of bundles of second type (as in case
(2-b)
of Lemma
3.2
below). Such a bundle
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to\omega_{C}(-D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and where
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
is general.

(D-ii) If either
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+3}{4}
,
2g-7+3\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu
or otherwise
g\geq 10
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}
,
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0\,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
whereas
N\in Pic^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
is general of its degree, so non-effective and special.

Moreover, the component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, when
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu
, whereas it is regular, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, if
d=2g-7+2\nu
.

(E) Concerning irreducible components of second type (in the sense of Definition
3.3
below), we have the following situation:

(E-i) For any
g\geqslant 7
,
3\leqslant\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
2g-2\leqslant d\leqslant 3g-7
, the Brill-Noether locus
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
contains a regular component, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
, which is uniruled. For
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
general, the corresponding bundle
\mathcal{F}
fits into an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0,

where
N\in\operatorname{Pic}^{d-2g+2}(C)
is general (special, non-effective), and
\mathcal{F}
arises from a general point
u
of an irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
of the degeneracy locus

\mathcal{W}_{2}:=\{u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)\ |\ {\rm corank}\left(H^{0}(\omega_{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{u}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(N)\right)\geq 2\}\subseteq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)

(cf. formula (
2.2
) below, where
\partial_{u}
denotes the natural coboundary map associated to the exact sequence defining
\mathcal{F}
), such that the component
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is of (expected) codimension
2d-10-4g
(cf. formula (
2.3
) below) and
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is such that
{\rm corank}(\partial_{u})=2
. Furthermore, the bundle also fits in the exact sequence

0\to N(p)\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-p)\to 0,

where
p\in C
general and
N(p)\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+3}(C)
general of its degree.

(E-ii) For any
g\geq 9
,
\frac{g+3}{4}\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
3g-6\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, the Brill-Noether locus
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
admits a regular component, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
, whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle
\mathcal{F}
of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which fits into an exact sequence of the form

0\to\omega_{C}\otimes G_{t}^{\vee}\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0,

where
|G_{t}|=g^{2}_{t}
is a base point free and birationally very ample linear system, corresponding to a general point
G_{t}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}\subseteq W^{2}_{t}(C)
, where
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}
the irreducible component
of the rank-
1
Brill-Noether locus
W^{2}_{t}(C)
as in Lemma
2.4
below, where necessarily
\frac{2}{3}g+2\leq t\leq g+2
, and where
\mathcal{F}
arises as an extension from
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C},K_{C}-g^{2}_{t})
corresponding to a general point of an irreducible component
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of the degeneracy locus
\mathcal{W}_{2}
similarly as above, such that
\mathcal{U}_{2}
is superabundant, of dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{U}_{2})=2
and such that
{\rm corank}(\partial_{u})=2
. Furthermore, the bundle
\mathcal{F}
also fits in the exact sequence

0\to\omega_{C}\otimes G_{t}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(p)\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-p)\to 0.

Proof.

The proof of the above result is given in full details in Sect.
3
. Precisely, Lemma
2.1
reminds that, for any
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
, there is no irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable vector bundle of speciality
j:=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})>3
. Therefore, one can focus on stable bundles of degree
d
and speciality exactly
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
.

The proof of (A) is then given in Proposition
3.1
whereas that of (B) in Proposition
3.5
. For what concerns case (C), taking into account Lemma
3.2
and Definition
3.3
below, the uniqueness among components of
first type
is a consequence of Propositions
3.7
,
3.8
-
(i)
,
3.12
,
3.30
and Lemma
3.26
; all the rest is proved in Proposition
3.13
. Finally, proof of (D) is conducted in Proposition
3.19
, whereas that of (E) in Propositions
3.32
and
3.42
.
∎

Remark 0.1
.

To sum-up,
Main Theorem
states that Brill-Noether loci
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
on a general
\nu
-gonal curve
C
have irreducible components as follows:

\bullet

{\underline{3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{6}}}

\mathcal{B}_{sup,(2-b),3}
: superabundant}

{
\mathcal{B}_{sup,(1-a),3}
: superabundant}
\frac{10}{3}g
-6
?

\nu

g
-7+2
\nu
2

{
\mathcal{B}_{reg,3}
: regular component}
\uparrow
{two regular components}
\uparrow
{two regular components+one superabundant}

\bullet

{\underline{\frac{g}{6}<\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}}}

d

g

\nu
{no comp.}
4
g

\nu

\mathcal{B}_{sup,(1-a),3}
: superabundant}

\mathcal{B}_{sup,(2-b),3}

\uparrow
{two regular components}
\uparrow

d

{no comp.}

\mathcal{B}_{sup,(1-a),3}
: superabundant}
\frac{10}{3}g

{
\mathcal{B}_{reg,3}
: regular component}

Our strategy proves furthermore that for some
d
the general fiber of a natural
determinant map
is frequently non-empty, even in cases where numerical expectations might suggest otherwise. More precisely, as a direct consequence of
Main Theorem
, we also obtain:

Main Corollary.

Take assumptions and notation as in
Main Theorem
and set
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general. Consider the natural surjective determinant map

\det:U_{C}(d)\to{\rm Pic}^{d}(C),\;\;[\mathcal{F}]\longrightarrow\det(\mathcal{F}).

Set

U_{C}(M):={\det}^{-1}(M),\;\;U_{C}^{s}(M):=U_{C}(M)\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)\;\;{\rm and}\;\;B_{M}^{k_{3}}:=\left\{[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(M)\;\;|\;\;h^{0}(\mathcal{F})\geqslant k_{3}\right\}.

One has the following situation.

\bullet
With numerical assumptions as in (C-ii) of
Main Theorem
,
the Brill-Noether locus
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is not empty, even when its expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d
is negative, which occurs for
d>3g-6
and
\nu<\frac{g+1}{4}
. More precisely, such a locus contains an irreducible component of first type, namely
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a),M}:=U^{s}_{C}(M)\cap\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}
, whose general point corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
as in (C-ii), with
\det(\mathcal{F})=K_{C}-2A+N=M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general.

Moreover
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a),M})=5g-6-4\nu-d
, which is of expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d
if and only if
d=2g-6+2\nu
, otherwise it is superabundant.

\bullet
With numerical assumptions as in (D-ii) of
Main Theorem
together with
d\leq 3g-4-\nu
, the Brill-Noether locus
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is not empty. More precisely, it contains an irreducible component of modified type, namely
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M}:=U^{s}_{C}(M)\cap\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
,
whose general point corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
as in (D-ii), with
\det(\mathcal{F})=K_{C}-A+N=M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general.

Moreover
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M})=7g-11-2\nu-2d
, which is the expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d\geq 0
if and only if
d=2g-7+2\nu
, otherwise
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M}
is superabundant.

Proof.

Consider the restriction of the determinant map

B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle det_{|}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
and its further restriction to the irreducible components constructed in
Main Theorem
.

Notice first that, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general, from
[
21
]
the
expected dimension
of
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=3g-3-3k_{3}=9g-18-3d
, which is negative if and only if
d>3g-6
.

For the component of
first type
as in
Main Theorem
-(C-ii), from the generality of
N
in
{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
we deduce that
\det(\mathcal{F})=N+K_{C}-2A
is also general in
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
. Therefore, the map
\det_{|_{\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}}}
is dominant onto
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
. Hence, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general, one has
\det_{|_{\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}}}^{-1}(M)\neq\emptyset
. Moreover, since

M=N+K_{C}-2A\cong M^{\prime}:=N^{\prime}+K_{C}-2A^{\prime}
if and only if
N\cong N^{\prime}
, one deduces the statement about the dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a),M})=5g-6-4\nu-d
. The fact that it is of the
expected dimension

\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d
if and only if
d=2g-6+2\nu
, otherwise it is
superabundant
, follows from the fact that
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}-g
, the dominance of
\det_{|_{\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}}}
and the fact that the component of
first type
as in
Main Theorem
-(C-ii) is
superabundant
unless
d=2g-6+2\nu
, when it is
regular
.

Similar proof for the case (D-ii) as in
Main Theorem
, dealing with components of
modified type
. On the other hand, differently from the previous case, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general the Brill-Noether locus
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
does not contain a component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M}
from case (D) when
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d<0
. Indeed, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general,
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
contains a component of modified type only from cases (D-ii) in
Main Theorem
; if such a component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M}
existed for
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d<0
, namely when
d>3g-6
which is contrary to the upper bound of
d\leq 3g-4-\nu
.

Finally, components of
second type
as in
Main Theorem
-(E) do not give rise to irreducible components of
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general; indeed in case (E-i) of
Main Theorem
we have
d\leq 3g-7
where
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d<0
, whereas in case (E-ii)
\det(\mathcal{F})=2K_{C}-G_{t}
is not general in
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
.
∎

1.
Preliminaries

In this paper we work over
\mathbb{C}
. All schemes will be endowed with the Zariski topology. We will interchangeably use the terms
rank-
r
vector bundle
on a smooth, projective variety
X
and
rank-
r
locally free sheaf
; for this reason we may sometimes abuse notation by identifying divisor classes with corresponding line bundles and interchangeably using additive and multiplicative notation.
The dual bundle of a rank-
r
vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
; if
L
is a line bundle, we therefore interchangeably use
L^{\vee}
or
-L
.

Given a smooth, projective variety
X
, we will denote by
\sim
the linear equivalence of Cartier divisors on
X
and by
\sim_{alg}
their algebraic equivalence. If
\mathcal{M}
is a moduli space, parametrizing objects modulo a given equivalence relation, and if
Y
is a representative of an equivalence class in
\mathcal{M}
, we denote by
[Y]\in\mathcal{M}
the point corresponding to
Y
.

If
C
denotes any smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g
, as customary,
W^{r}_{d}(C)
will denote the
Brill-Noether locus
as in the Introduction,
parametrizing line bundles
L\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
such that
h^{0}(L)\geq r+1
. The integer

\rho(g,r,d):=g-(r+1)(g+r-d)

denotes the
Brill-Noether number
and the multiplication map

\mu_{L}:H^{0}(C,L)\otimes H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes L^{\vee})\to H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})

denotes the so called
Petri map
of the line bundle
L
. As for the rest, we will use standard terminology and notation as in
[
2
,
18
]
.

1.1.
Brill-Noether theory of line bundles on general
\nu
-gonal curves

Here we briefly review basic results in Brill-Noether theory of line bundles on a general
\nu
-gonal curve, which will be used in the sequel. From the Introduction, given a positive integer
g
, from now on
\nu
will be an integer such that

(1.1)

3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor,\;{\rm(in\;particular}\;g\geq 4).

We will say that a smooth, irreducible, projective curve
C
is a

general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
if
[C]
is a general point of the
\nu
-gonal stratum

\mathcal{M}^{1}_{g,\nu}\subsetneq\mathcal{M}_{g}
, where
\mathcal{M}^{1}_{g,\nu}
is the image via the natural modular map
\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{M}_{g}
where
\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}
the
Hurwitz scheme
as in the Introduction. We first remind the following:

Proposition 1.1

(cf.
[
3
]
)
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
, let
r
be a non-negative integer and let
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
be the unique line bundle on
C
associated to the unique base-point-free pencil on
C
of degree
\nu
, i.e.
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
. If
g\geq r(\nu-1)
, then
\operatorname{dim}(|A^{\otimes r}|)=r
whereas if
g<r(\nu-1)
then
A^{\otimes r}
is non-special hence
\operatorname{dim}(|A^{\otimes r}|)=r\,\nu-g>r
.

Notice further that the unique
g^{1}_{\nu}
on
C
induces a morphism
f:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. For any
L\in\text{Pic}^{d}(C)
, the pushforward
f_{*}(L)
is a rank-
\nu
bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
which, by Grothendieck’s Theorem, decomposes as:

f_{*}(L)\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{i}),\quad\text{with }e_{1}\leq\dots\leq e_{\nu}\;\text{ and }\;\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}e_{i}=d+1-g-\nu.

The
splitting type vector

\vec{e}:=(e_{1},\dots,e_{\nu})
provides a refinement of the classical Brill-Noether loci. Indeed, one defines the
splitting locus
and its closure:

W^{\vec{e}}(C):=\{L\in\text{Pic}^{d}(C)\mid f_{*}(L)\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e})\},\quad\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}:=\bigcup_{\vec{e}\,^{\prime}\leq\vec{e}}W^{\vec{e}\,^{\prime}}

where
\leq
denotes the standard partial ordering on partitions. The expected codimension of
W^{\vec{e}}(C)
is the
magnitude

u(\vec{e}):=h^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{E}nd(f_{*}(L))=\sum_{i<j}\operatorname{max}\{0,e_{j}-e_{i}-1\}

cf. e.g.
[
23
]
. It turns out that the classical Brill-Noether locus
W_{d}^{r}(C)
decomposes into the union of splitting loci, namely

(1.2)

W_{d}^{r}(C)\ =\bigcup_{\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{e}\;{\rm maximal\;s.t.}\;|\vec{e}|=d-g+1+\nu\\
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))\geq r+1\end{array}}\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}.

Characterizing contributions of some splitting loci to the irreducible components of
W_{d}^{r}(C)
is therefore equivalent to
determining those splitting type vectors that are maximal w.r.t.
\leq
among those satisfying:

(1.3)

\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}e_{i}=d+1-g-\nu\;\;{\rm and}\;\;h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}{\rm max}\,\{0,\,e_{i}+1\}\geq r+1.

These are the so-called

“balanced plus balanced”
splitting type vectors in
[
23
, p. 769]
, uniquely determined by the number of their non-negative components. In this set-up, one has

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g\geq 4
and let
r
and
d
be non-negative integers.

(1)
If
r\leq d-g
, then
W_{d}^{r}(C)={\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
;

(2)
If
r>d-g
, set
d^{\prime}:=d+1-g-\nu
. Then:

(2-i)

a splitting type vector
\vec{e}
, which is maximal among those satisfying (
1.3
), is such that
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}{\rm max}\,\{0,\,e_{i}+1\}=r+1
(cf.
[
14
, Lemma 2.7]
);

(2-ii)

maximal splitting type vectors as in (2-i) are vectors
\vec{w}_{r,\ell}
such that

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=B(\nu-r-1+\ell,\;d^{\prime}-\ell)\oplus B(r+1-\ell,\;\ell),

where as above
B(-,\;-)
denotes the unique balanced bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of given rank and degree, respectively, for

{\rm max}\;\{0,\;r+2-\nu\}\leq\ell\leq r
an integer such that either
\ell=0
or
\ell\leq g+2r+1-d-\nu
. Moreover,

u(\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=g-\left(\rho(g,r-\ell,d)-\ell\,\nu\right)\ {\mbox{ (cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Lar}{}{}, Lemma\,2.2]})}}.

(2-iii)

for any integer
\ell
as in (2-ii), the corresponding splitting type vector
\vec{w}_{r,\ell}
is the unique
degree–
d^{\prime}
, rank–
\nu
vector with
r+1-\ell
non-negative coordinates, which is maximal among those with
r+1
linearly independent global sections (cf.
[
23
, Cor. 1.3]
);

(2-iv)

Every component of
W_{d}^{r}(C)
is generically smooth, of (expected) dimension

\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{r,\ell}):=g-u(\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=\rho(g,r-\ell,d)-\ell\,\nu,

for some integer
{\rm max}\;\{0,\;r+2-\nu\}\leq\ell\leq r
, where
\rho(g,r-\ell,d)
the classical Brill-Noether number, such that either
\ell=0
or
\ell\leq g+2r+1-d-\nu
. Such a component exists for each integer
\ell
as above satisfying furthermore
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{r,\ell})\geq 0
(cf.
[
23
, Cor. 1.3]
).

1.2.
Ruled surfaces and families of special unisecants

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-two vector bundle of degree
d
on
C
. We denote by
\pi:F:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\to C
the associated ruled surface (or
surface scroll
). Let
H
be a divisor in the class of the tautological line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)
, such that
H^{2}=\mbox{deg}(\det\mathcal{F})=d
. The speciality of the bundle (and of the scroll
F
) is defined as
i(\mathcal{F}):=h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})
.

Divisors
\widetilde{\Gamma}
on
F
such that
\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})\cong\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\otimes\pi^{*}(N^{\vee})
for some
N\in\text{Pic}(C)
are termed
unisecants
of
F
. Setting
f_{D}:=\pi^{*}(D)
, for any
D\in\text{Div}(C)
, a unisecant satisfies
\widetilde{\Gamma}\sim H-f_{D}
, with degree
\mbox{deg}(\widetilde{\Gamma})=d-\mbox{deg}(D)
. Irreducible unisecants correspond to
sections
of
F
and are isomorphic to
C
. For a positive integer
\delta
, we denote by
\text{Div}_{F}^{1,\delta}
the
Hilbert scheme of unisecants of degree

\delta
on
F
. Any unisecant
\widetilde{\Gamma}
of degree
\delta\leq d
admits a decomposition
\widetilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma+f_{D}
, where
\Gamma
is a section and
D\geq 0
an effective divisor on
C
. This gives rise to

(1.4)

0\to N(-D)\to\mathcal{F}\to L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{D}\to 0;

in particular if
D=0
, i.e.

\widetilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma
is a section of
F
, then
\mathcal{F}
fits in

(1.5)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to L\to 0

and the
normal bundle
of
\Gamma
in
F
is:

(1.6)

\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma/F}\simeq L\otimes N^{\vee},\;\;{\rm so}\;\;\Gamma^{2}=\mbox{deg}(L)-\mbox{deg}(N)=2\delta-d

(cf. e.g.
[
12
, Rem. 2.1]
). Accordingly,
{\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta}
is endowed with the structure of Quot scheme; namely (cf.
[
32
, § 4.4]
) one has

(1.7)

{\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Quot}^{C}_{\mathcal{F},\delta+t-g+1},\;\;\;\;\widetilde{\Gamma}\to\left\{\mathcal{F}\to\!\!\to L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{D}\right\}.

As such, one has

H^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}/F})\simeq T_{[\widetilde{\Gamma}]}({\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta})\simeq{\rm Hom}(N(-D),L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{D})\;\;{\rm and}\;\;H^{1}(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}/F})\simeq{\rm Ext}^{1}(N(-D),L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{D}).

Finally, if
\widetilde{\Gamma}\sim H-f_{D}
on
F
, for some effective divisor
D
on
C
, then one has

(1.8)

|\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})|\simeq\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\mathcal{F}(-D))).

Definition 1.3
.

(cf.
[
12
, Def. 2.7]
) A unisecant
\widetilde{\Gamma}\in{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
is said to be:

(a) linearly isolated (li) if
\operatorname{dim}(|\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})|)=0
;

(b) algebraically isolated (ai) if
\operatorname{dim}({\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F})=0
.

For a unisecant
\widetilde{\Gamma}
as in (
1.4
), by (
1.8
), one has

\widetilde{\Gamma}\in|\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\otimes\rho^{*}(N^{\vee}(D))|
so, applying
\rho_{*}
and Leray’s isomorphism, one gets

(1.9)

i(\widetilde{\Gamma})=h^{1}(L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{D})=h^{1}(L)=i(\Gamma),

where
\Gamma
denotes the unique section contained in
\widetilde{\Gamma}
.

In general, speciality is not constant either in linear systems or in algebraic families of unisecants (cf. e.g.
[
12
, Examples 2.8, 2.9]
); nevertheless, since
{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
has a Quot-scheme structure there is a universal quotient
\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta}\to{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
so, taking
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta}):={\rm Proj}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
, one can consider

(1.10)

\mathcal{S}_{F}^{1,\delta}:=\{\widetilde{\Gamma}\in{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}\;\;|\;\;R^{1}p_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta})}(1))_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\neq 0\},

i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{F}^{1,\delta}
is the support of
R^{1}p_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta})}(1))
, which parametrizes degree-
\delta
, special unisecants of
F
. In this set-up, we recall the following results which will be sometimes used in the sequel.

Proposition 1.4
.

(cf.
[
12
, Lemma 2.11, Prop. 2.12]
) (1) Let
\Gamma\subset F
be a section corresponding to the exact sequence (
1.5
). A section
\Gamma^{\prime}
, corresponding to a quotient
\mathcal{F}\to\!\!\!\!\!\to L^{\prime}
, is such that

\Gamma\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
on
F
if and only if
L\simeq L^{\prime}
. In particular
i(\Gamma)=i(\Gamma^{\prime})
.

(2) Assume
\mathcal{F}
to be non-splitting and let
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{F}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{1,\delta}_{F}
be a section of
F
, where
\mathfrak{F}
is an irreducible, projective scheme of dimension
k
, parametrizing a flat family of unisecants. Assume that:

(2-i) either
k\geq 1
, if
\mathfrak{F}
is a linear system;

(2-ii) otherwise, if
\mathfrak{F}
is not a linear family, assume either
k\geq 2
or
k=1
and
\mathfrak{F}
with base points.

Then,
\mathfrak{F}
contains reducible unisecants
\widetilde{\Gamma}\subset F
such that
i(\widetilde{\Gamma})\geq i(\Gamma)
.

1.3.
Moduli spaces of rank-two (semi)stable bundles on curves and Brill-Nother loci

Recall that a rank-two vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
on a smooth curve
C
is
stable
(resp.
semistable
) if for every sub-line bundle
N\subset\mathcal{F}
, its
slope
satisfies
\mu(N)<\mu(\mathcal{F})
(resp.
\mu(N)\leq\mu(\mathcal{F})
), where
\mu(\mathcal{E}):=\frac{\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{E})}{\text{rk}(\mathcal{E})}
.

The moduli space
U_{C}(d)
, parametrizing (S-equivalence classes of) semistable bundles of rank two and degree
d
, is an irreducible variety of dimension
4g-3
. Within this space,
U_{C}^{s}(d)
denotes the open, dense subset of isomorphism classes of stable bundles whereas the boundary
U_{C}(d)\setminus U_{C}^{s}(d)
, if not empty, contains strictly semistable bundles (cf. e.g.
[
28
,
31
]
).

Proposition 1.5
.

(cf. e.g.
[
12
, Prop. 3.1]
) Let
C
be a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 1
and let
d
be an integer.

(i)
If
d\geq 4g-3
, then for any
[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)
, one has
i(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

(ii)
If
g\geq 2
and
d\geq 2g-2
, for
[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)
general, one has
i(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

From Proposition
1.5
, Serre duality and invariance of (semi)stability under operations like tensoring with a line bundle or passing to the dual bundle, for
g\geq 2
it makes sense to consider the proper sub-loci of
U_{C}(d)
parametrizing classes
[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)
such that
i(\mathcal{F})>0
in the following range for
d
:

(1.11)

2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4.

Definition 1.6
.

Given
C
any smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 2
and considered
d
and
i
two non-negative integers, we set
k_{i}:=d-2g+2+i
and define

(1.12)

B^{k_{i}}_{d}:=\left\{[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)\,|\,h^{0}(\mathcal{F})\geq k_{i}\right\}=\left\{[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)\,|\,h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=i(\mathcal{F})\geq i\right\},

the
k_{i}^{th}
–Brill-Noether locus,
simply Brill-Noether locus, when no confusion arises.

Remark 1.7
.

Brill-Noether loci
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
have a natural structure of (locally) determinantal subschemes of
U_{C}(d)
:

(a)
When
d
is odd,
U_{C}(d)=U^{s}_{C}(d)
, then
U_{C}(d)
is a fine moduli space and the existence of a universal bundle on
C\times U_{C}(d)
allows one to construct
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
as the degeneracy locus of a morphism between suitable vector bundles on
U_{C}(d)
(see, e.g.
[
17
]
for details). Accordingly, the
expected dimension
of
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
is

{\rm max}\{-1,\ \rho_{d}^{k_{i}}\}
, where

(1.13)

\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}:=4g-3-ik_{i}

is the rank-
2

Brill-Noether number
as in the Introduction. If
\emptyset\neq B^{k_{i}}_{d}\subsetneq U_{C}(d)
,
then
B^{k_{i}+1}_{d}\subseteq{\rm Sing}(B^{k_{i}}_{d})
.

Since any
[\mathcal{F}]\in B^{k_{i}}_{d}
corresponds to a stable bundle, it is a smooth point of
U_{C}(d)
and the Zariski tangent space
T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(U_{C}(d))
at
[\mathcal{F}]
to
U_{C}(d)
identifies to
H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})^{\vee}
. If
[\mathcal{F}]\in B^{k_{i}}_{d}\setminus B^{k_{i}+1}_{d}
,
the tangent space to
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
at
[\mathcal{F}]
is otherwise the
annihilator
of the image of the
Petri map
of
\mathcal{F}
(see, e.g.
[
35
]
)

(1.14)

\mu_{\mathcal{F}}:H^{0}(C,\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(C,\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\longrightarrow H^{0}(C,\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}).

If
[\mathcal{F}]\in B^{k_{i}}_{d}\setminus B^{k_{i}+1}_{d}
, then

\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}=h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})-h^{0}(C,\mathcal{F})h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})
moreover
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
is non–singular and of the expected dimension at
[\mathcal{F}]
if and only if the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
is injective.

(b)
When otherwise
d
is even,
U_{C}(d)
is not a fine moduli space (because
U^{ss}_{C}(d)\neq\emptyset
). There is a suitable open, non-empty subscheme
\mathcal{Q}^{ss}\subset\mathcal{Q}
of a certain Quot scheme
\mathcal{Q}
defining
U_{C}(d)
via a GIT-quotient map
\pi
(cf. e.g.
[
37
]
for details) and one can define
B^{k_{i}}_{C}(d)
as the image via
\pi
of the degeneracy locus of a morphism between suitable vector bundles on
\mathcal{Q}^{ss}
. It may happen for a component
\mathcal{B}
of a Brill–Noether locus to be totally contained in
U_{C}^{ss}(d)
;
in this case the lower bound
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
for the expected dimension of
\mathcal{B}
is no longer valid (cf. e.g.

[
8
, Remark 7.4]
). Nonetheless, the lower bound
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
is still valid if
\mathcal{B}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)\neq\emptyset
.

Recall, finally, the following terminology.

Definition 1.8
.

Assume
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\neq\emptyset
. A component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{i}}

such that
\mathcal{B}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)\neq\emptyset
will be called regular, if it is generically smooth and of
(expected) dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B})=\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
,
superabundant, otherwise.

1.4.
Line bundle extensions and (semi)stability via Segre invariant

For a rank-two vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
of degree
d
on a curve
C
, the
Segre invariant

s(\mathcal{F})
is:

s(\mathcal{F}):=\min_{N\subset\mathcal{F}}\{\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})-2\mbox{deg}(N)\},

where
N
ranges over all sub-line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
. This invariant is stable under twisting by line bundles and by dualization. A sub-line bundle
N
computing
s(\mathcal{F})
is a
maximal degree
sub-line bundle of
\mathcal{F}
, consequently its corresponding quotient
L=\mathcal{F}/N
is a
minimal degree
quotient line bundle of
\mathcal{F}
. In terms of the surface scroll
F:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
as in Sect.
1.2
, a section
\Gamma\in\text{Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
of minimal degree
\delta
satisfies
\Gamma^{2}=s(\mathcal{F})=2\delta-d
; hence, geometrically stability of
\mathcal{F}
is entirely determined by the Segre invariant, indeed
\mathcal{F}
is semistable iff
s(\mathcal{F})\geq 0
, whereas it is stable iff
s(\mathcal{F})>0
.

Let now
\delta\leq d
be a positive integer; consider
L\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{\delta}(C)
and
N\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-\delta}(C)
. Any
u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
gives rise to a degree-
d
, rank-
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
, fitting in:

(u):\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to L\to 0.

If
\Gamma
is the section on the associated surface scroll
F_{u}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
corresponding to the surjection
\mathcal{F}_{u}\to\!\!\to L
then, since
\Gamma^{2}=\mbox{deg}(L-N)
, a necessary condition for
\mathcal{F}_{u}
to be semistable is therefore

(1.15)

\Gamma^{2}=2\delta-d\geq s(\mathcal{F}_{u})\geq 0.

In such a case, Riemann-Roch theorem gives

(1.16)

\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=\begin{cases}\ 2\delta-d+g-1\ &\text{ if }L\ncong N\\
\ g\ &\text{ if }L\simeq N.\end{cases}

Proposition 1.9
.

(cf.
[
4
, Prop. 1]
) Let
C
be a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 3
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-
2
vector bundle on
C
of degree
d\geq 2g-2
, with speciality
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})\geq 2
. Then
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
for
u\in{\rm Ext^{1}}(\omega_{C}(-D),\,N)
, namely
\mathcal{F}
arises as an extension of the form

(1.17)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}\to\omega_{C}(-D)\to 0,

where
D
is an effective divisor on
C
such that either
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=1
or
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=i
.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is the “Serre-dual” version of
[
4
, Lemma 1]
or even of
[
35
, Cor. 1.2]
, cited in the latter
as a direct consequence on an unpublished result of B. Feinberg; this result has been completely proved in
[
4
, Prop. 1]
. The last part of the statement is a direct consequence of (semi)stability and (
1.16
).
∎

Definition 1.10
.

A rank-
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
with speciality
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})\geq 2
, fitting in (
1.17
) and which satisfies
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=i
is said to be a bundle of first type; otherwise if
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=1
then
\mathcal{F}
is said to be a bundle of second type (cf.
[
4
, Definition 1]
).

We will construct irreducible components
\mathcal{B}
of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, with the use of the above subdivision in bundles of
first
or
second type
for the general point
[\mathcal{F}]
of any such a component
\mathcal{B}
, or even by performing some natural
modifications
of these two types of presentations, when such a variation let the construction of a prospected irreducible component
\mathcal{B}
to be easier (cf. e.g. Proposition
3.19
below). To this aim, in order to characterize the stability of rank-two bundles constructed as extensions, we may use geometric criteria established by Lange and Narasimhan in
[
20
]
as reminded below.

Consider a bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
defined by an extension:

(1.18)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to L\to 0,

where
u\in H^{1}(C,N\otimes L^{\vee})\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\mathbb{P}:=\mathbb{P}(H^{1}(C,N\otimes L^{\vee}))
. Assuming the linear system
|K_{C}\otimes L\otimes N^{\vee}|
induces a morphism
\varphi:C\to\mathbb{P}
, we let
X=\varphi(C)
denote the image curve. The stability of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
will be governed by the position of the extension class
[u]
relative to the
secant varieties
of
X
.

Precisely, recall that the
h
-th secant variety
of
X
,
\text{Sec}_{h}(X)\subseteq\mathbb{P}
, is defined to be the closure of the union of linear spans
\langle\varphi(D)\rangle
, for all degree-
h
effective divisors
D\in C^{(h)}
. In this set-up, we have:

Proposition 1.11
.

(
[
20
, Proposition 1.1]
) Assume
2\delta-d\geq 2
. Then
\varphi=\varphi_{|K_{C}+L-N|}:C\to\mathbb{P}
is a morphism. Moreover, for any integer

\sigma\equiv 2\delta-d\ \text{ (mod\ 2) }
such that
4+d-2\delta\leq\sigma\leq 2\delta-d
, one has
s(\mathcal{F}_{u})\geq\sigma
if and only if
[u]\notin\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d+\sigma-2)}(X)
, where
X=\varphi(C)\subset\mathbb{P}
.

Consequently,
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable if and only if
[u]
lies outside specific secant varieties that would violate the condition
s(\mathcal{F}_{u})>0
.

2.
Auxiliary initial results for the proof of
Main Theorem

In this section we introduce preliminaries which are needed for the proof of
Main Theorem
. From now on, unless otherwise stated, we will therefore consider
C
to be a
general
\nu
-gonal curve
of genus
g\geq 4
and, as in (
1.1
) and (
1.11
), we will set

3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4.

To prove
Main Theorem
recall the following:

Lemma 2.1
.

(cf. e.g.
[
24
, Lemme 2.6 and pp.101–102]
) Let
C
be any smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g
. For any integer
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
and
i>0
there is no irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
whose general member
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to rank-
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
of speciality
j:=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})>i
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that in
[
24
, pp.101-102]
for
B^{0}_{d}
,
1\leq d\leq 2g-2
, which uses
elementary transformations
of vector bundles.
∎

For a general point
[\mathcal{F}]
of an irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, the bundle must be stable with degree
d
and speciality
i(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})=3
. By Proposition
1.9
, such bundles arise as non-split extensions:

(2.1)

(u)\>\>0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to L\to 0,

where
L\cong\omega_{C}(-D)
is an effective, special line bundle of degree
\delta>\frac{d}{2}
. Following Definition
1.10
, we classify
\mathcal{F}
based on the speciality of
L
:
first type
if
h^{1}(L)=3
,
second type
if
h^{1}(L)=1
.

These categories allow us to identify potential obstructions to the existence of components (cf. Propositions
3.7
,
3.8
,
3.12
,
3.30
). Otherwise, in the absence of obstructions, we employ a parametric approach to construct these components and analyze their infinitesimal structure—specifically, the kernel of the associated Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
. While the primary classification focuses on specialities
1
and
3
, we occasionally utilize line bundles
L
with
h^{1}(L)=2
. This
modified type
approach (cf. Proposition
3.19
) simplifies the construction of certain loci and the study of their local geometry.

Our analysis integrates the study of degeneracy loci in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
with the Brill–Noether theory of rank-one loci
W^{r}_{t}(C)
for
r=1,2
on general
\nu
-gonal curves. Precisely, for any exact sequence
(u)
as in (
2.1
), if one sets
H^{0}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{u}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(N)
the associated
coboundary map
then, for any integer
t>0
, one can define the
degeneracy locus
:

(2.2)

\mathcal{W}_{t}:=\{u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)\ |\ {\rm corank}(\partial_{u})\geq t\}\subseteq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N),

which has a natural structure of (locally) determinantal scheme and, as such, it has an
expected codimension
, which is

(2.3)

c(t):={\rm max}\{0,\;t(h^{0}(L)-h^{1}(N)+t)\}

(cf. e.g.
[
12
, § 5.2]
for details). In this set–up, one has:

Theorem 2.2
.

(
[
12
, Thm. 5.8, Cor. 5.9]
) Let
C
be a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 3
.
Let
0\leq\delta\leq d
be integers and let
L\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{\delta}(C)
be special and effective and
N\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-\delta}(C)
.
Set
l:=h^{0}(L)
,
r:=h^{1}(N)
,
m:=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))
and assume
r\geq 1,\,l\geq\operatorname{max}\{1,r-1\},\,m\geq l+1
. Then:

(i)

\mathcal{W}_{1}
as in (
2.2
) is irreducible of the expected dimension

m-c(1)=m-(l-r+1)
;

(ii)
if
l\geq r
, then
\mathcal{W}_{1}\subsetneq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
and, for general
u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
, the associated coboundary map
\partial_{u}
is surjective whereas, for general
w\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
, one has
{\rm corank}(\partial_{w})=1
.

To study instead rank-one Brill-Noether loci of interests on
C
a general
\nu
-gonal curve, we will use Theorem
1.2
to prove the next results.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
g\geq 4
and
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
be integers. Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
and let
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
be the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
.
Let
t\geq\nu
be an integer and, for a given integer
0\leq\ell\leq 1
, let

\vec{w}_{1,\ell}
be the corresponding maximal splitting type vector and

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{1,\ell})
be the associated balanced-plus-balanced bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as in Theorem
1.2
. Then one has:

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{1,0})=B(\nu-2,\,t+1-g-\nu)\oplus B(2,\,0)\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{1,1})=B(\nu-1,\,t+1-g-\nu)\oplus B(1,\,1),

where

B(-,-)
denotes the most balanced bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of given
({\rm rank},\;{\rm degree})
, therefore
B(2,0)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus 2}
,
B(1,1)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)
whereas the other summands are bundles of negative degrees. Moreover,

W^{1}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}&\mbox{ if $\nu\leq t<\frac{g+2}{2}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}&\mbox{ if $\frac{g+2}{2}\leq t\leq g+2-\nu$ }\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}&\mbox{ if $g-\nu+2<t<g+1$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+1$}\end{cases}

and, when
W^{1}_{t}(C)\subsetneq{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)
, any of its irreducible components is generically smooth of dimension

\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}})=t-\nu\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}})=\rho(g,1,t)=2t-g-2.

Furthermore:

(i)
a general element in
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}
is given by
\mathcal{L}_{1,1}:=A\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(B_{t-\nu})
, where
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and
B_{t-\nu}=p_{1}+\cdots+p_{t-\nu}
the effective divisor of base points of
|\mathcal{L}_{1,1}|
;

(ii)
a general element in
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}
is given by
\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
, where
|\mathcal{L}_{1,0}|
is a base-point-free, complete
g^{1}_{t}
.

Proof.

If
t\geq g+1
, then
W^{1}_{t}(C)=\text{Pic}^{t}(C)
by Riemann-Roch. For
t<g+1
, we identify the maximal splitting type vectors
\vec{w}_{1,\ell}
using Theorem
1.2
. The admissible range for
\ell
is
0\leq\ell\leq 1
. Specifically, if
t\leq g+2-\nu
, both
\ell=0
and
\ell=1
occur whereas, if
g+2-\nu<t<g+1
, only
\ell=0
occurs. Setting
t^{\prime}:=t+1-g-\nu
, we analyze the resulting components:

(i) For
(r,\ell)=(1,1)
, we have
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{1,1})=B(\nu-1,t^{\prime}-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)
. The component exists if
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{1,1})=t-\nu\geq 0
. A dimension count shows that its general element is of the form
A\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(p_{1}+\dots+p_{t-\nu})
, where
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
.

(ii) For
(r,\ell)=(1,0)
, we have
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{1,0})=B(\nu-2,t^{\prime})\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus 2}
. The component has dimension
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{1,0})=\rho(g,1,t)=2t-g-2
. To show that the general element
\mathcal{L}_{1,0}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}
is base-point-free, suppose otherwise. Then
\mathcal{L}_{1,0}(-p)\in W^{1}_{t-1}(C)
would share the same splitting type
B(\nu-2,t^{\prime}-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus 2}
. This implies
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}})\leq\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}_{t}-1})+1
, which contradicts the fact that
\rho(g,1,t)=\rho(g,1,t-1)+2
. Thus,
\mathcal{L}_{1,0}
is base-point-free.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
g\geq 4
and
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
be integers. Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
and let
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
be the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
.
Let
t\geq\nu
be an integer and, for a given integer
0\leq\ell\leq 2
, let

\vec{w}_{2,\ell}
denote the corresponding maximal splitting type vector and

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{2,\ell})
be the associated balanced-plus-balanced bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as in Theorem
1.2
. Then one has:

\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{2,0})=&B(\nu-3,\,t+1-g-\nu)\oplus B(3,\,0),\\
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{2,1})=&B(\nu-2,\,t-g-\nu)\oplus B(2,\,1),\\
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{2,2})=&B(\nu-1,\,t-1-g-\nu)\oplus B(1,\,2)\end{cases}

where
B(-,-)
denotes the most balanced bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of given
({\rm rank},\;{\rm degree})
, therefore
B(3,0)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}^{\oplus 3}
,
B(2,1)=\mathcal{\mathcal{}}O_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)
and
B(1,2)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)
, whereas the other summands are bundles of negative degrees. Moreover:

(i)
for
\nu=3
, one has respectively:

for
g=4

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $3\leq t<\frac{g+5}{2}=\frac{9}{2}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $\frac{2g+6}{3}=\frac{14}{3}\leq t=5<6=2\nu$ }\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq 6=g+2$}\end{cases}

for
g=5

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $3\leq t<\frac{g+5}{2}=\frac{10}{2}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}&\mbox{if $t=5=\frac{g+5}{2}<\frac{2g+6}{3}=\frac{16}{3}$ }\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $t=6=2\nu\ <7=g+2$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq 7=g+2$}\end{cases}

for
g=6

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $3\leq t<\frac{g+5}{2}=\frac{11}{2}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $2\nu=\frac{2g+6}{3}=6\leq t<8=g+2$ }\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq 8=g+2$}\end{cases}

for
g=7
,

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $3\leq t<6=2\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}&\mbox{if $t=6=2\nu=\frac{g+5}{2}<\frac{2g+6}{3}=\frac{20}{3}$ }\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $\frac{2g+6}{3}<7\leq t<9=g+2$ }\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq 9=g+2$}\end{cases}

whereas, for
g\geq 8
,

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $3\leq t<6=2\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}&\mbox{if $6=2\nu\leq t<\frac{g+5}{2}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}&\mbox{if $\frac{g+5}{2}\leq t<\frac{2g+6}{3}$ }\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $\frac{2g+6}{3}\leq t<g+2$ }\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+2$}\end{cases}

(ii)
for
4\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+2}{3}
, one has:

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $\nu\leq t<2\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}&\mbox{if $2\nu\leq t<\frac{g+2+\nu}{2}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}&\mbox{if $\frac{g+2+\nu}{2}\leq t<\frac{2g+6}{3}$ }\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $\frac{2g+6}{3}\leq t\leq g+3-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $t=g+4-\nu$ }\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $g+5-\nu\leq t<g+2$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+2$}\end{cases}

(iii)
for
4\leq\nu=\frac{g+3}{3}
, one has:

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $\nu\leq t<2\nu=g+3-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $t=2\nu=g+3-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\ \cup\ \overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $t=g+4-\nu$ }\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $g+5-\nu\leq t<g+2$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+2$}\end{cases}

(iv)
for
4\leq\nu=\frac{g+4}{3}
, one has:

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $\nu\leq t<g+3-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}&\mbox{if $t=g+3-\nu=\frac{g+2+\nu}{2}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $t=2\nu=g+4-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $g+5-\nu\leq t<g+2$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+2$}\end{cases}

(v)
for
4\leq\frac{g+4}{3}<\nu\leq\frac{g+6}{3}
, one has:

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $\nu\leq t\leq g+3-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\cup\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $t=g+4-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $g+5-\nu\leq t<g+2$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+2$}\end{cases}

(vi)
for
4\leq\frac{g+6}{3}<\nu\leq\frac{g+9}{3}
, one has:

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $\nu\leq t\leq g+4-\nu$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $g+5-\nu\leq t<g+2$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+2$}\end{cases}

(vii)
for
4\leq\frac{g+9}{3}<\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
, one has:

W^{2}_{t}(C)=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\mbox{if $\nu\leq t<\frac{2g+6}{3}$}\\
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}&\mbox{if $\frac{2g+6}{3}\leq t<g+2$}\\
{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)&\mbox{if $t\geq g+2$}\end{cases}

Furthermore, when
\emptyset\neq W^{2}_{t}(C)\subsetneq{\rm Pic}^{t}(C)
, any of its irreducible components as above is generically smooth of the following dimensions:

\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}})=t-2\nu,\;\;\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}})=2t-g-\nu-2\;{\rm and}\;\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}})=3t-2g-6,

and

(a) a general element in
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}
is given by
\mathcal{L}_{2,2}:=A^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(B_{t-2\nu})
, where
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and
B_{t-2\nu}=p_{1}+\cdots+p_{t-2\nu}
an effective divisor of base points of
|\mathcal{L}_{2,2}|
;

(b) a general element in
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
is given by
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}:=A\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M_{t-\nu})
, where
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and
M_{t-\nu}=p_{1}+\cdots+p_{t-\nu}\in C^{(t-\nu)}
an effective divisor of movable points such that
|\mathcal{L}_{2,1}|
is a base-point-free
and birationally very ample linear system
g^{2}_{t}
, that is, the morphism associated to the
g^{2}_{t}
is a birational map onto its image.

(c) a general element in
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}
is given by
\mathcal{L}_{2,0}
, where
|\mathcal{L}_{2,0}|
is a base-point-free and birationally very ample linear system
g^{2}_{t}
, that is, its associated morphism is a birational map onto its image.

Proof.

The proof of the first part of the statement is similar to that of Lemma
2.3
; it simply deals with taking into consideration conditions given by Theorem
1.2
so the details can be left to the interested reader.

For what concerns the description of the general element
\mathcal{L}_{2,\ell}
in any irreducible component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,\ell}}}
,
0\leq\ell\leq 2
, the reasoning is as follows.

(a)
Case
\ell=2
: since
g\geq 2(\nu-1)
, Proposition
1.1
implies
h^{0}(A^{\otimes 2})=3
, thus
A^{\otimes 2}\in W^{2}_{2\nu}(C)
. A general element is of the form
A^{\otimes 2}(p_{1}+\cdots+p_{t-2\nu})
. The result follows from the fact that
\operatorname{dim}(C^{(t-2\nu)})=t-2\nu=\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}})
, and the splitting type of its pushforward is maximal.

(b)
Case
\ell=1
: let
g^{2}_{t}=|\mathcal{L}_{2,1}|
. If
g^{2}_{t}
had a base point
p
, then
g^{2}_{t}(-p)
would belong to a component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}_{*}\subset W^{2}_{t-1}(C)
with splitting type
B(\nu-2,t^{\prime}-2)\oplus B(2,1)
. This would imply
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}})\leq\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}_{*})+1
, contradicting the actual dimension obtained by Lemma
2.3
. Thus, general
g^{2}_{t}
is base-point-free. To prove its birational very-ampleness, let
\varphi:C\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the induced map of degree
m
. If
m>1
, the dimension of the corresponding sublocus in
\mathcal{M}_{g}
is strictly less than the dimension of the
\nu
-gonal stratum, unless the geometric genus
\gamma
of the image is
0
. However,
\gamma=0
implies
g^{2}_{t}
is composed with a
g^{1}_{m}
. For
t<g+2
, a general
\nu
-gonal curve only admits pencils composed with the unique
g^{1}_{\nu}
(cf.
[
1
]
), forcing
g^{2}_{t}=2g^{1}_{\nu}
, a contradiction to the generality of
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
. Thus,
m=1
.

(c)
Case
\ell=0
: for
g^{2}_{t}=|\mathcal{L}_{2,0}|
, the base-point-free property follows from a dimension count:
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}})=3t-2g-6
, while
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}_{*})=3t-2g-9
which precludes existence of base points. Birational very ampleness follows by the same argument used in
(b)
.
∎

3.
Proof of
Main Theorem

In this section, we construct the irreducible components of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
for
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
to establish the
Main Theorem
. From (
1.13
), the expected dimension of any such component is:

\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d.

By Lemma
2.1
, a general point
[\mathcal{F}]
in any component
\mathcal{B}
satisfies
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
. By Proposition
1.9
, we analyze stability-driven extensions (
1.17
) via effective quotient line bundles
L=\omega_{C}(-D)
of degree
\delta>\frac{d}{2}
.

We will identify existence obstructions (cf. Props.
3.7
,
3.8
,
3.12
,
3.30
) by classifying
\mathcal{F}
according to the speciality of
L
:
first type bundles

\mathcal{F}
for
h^{1}(L)=3
and
second type bundles
when
h^{1}(L)=1
. In the absence of obstructions, we employ a parametric approach to construct these components and analyze their infinitesimal structure (specifically the kernel of the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
). To facilitate this construction and the study of local geometry, we occasionally use quotient line-bundles
L
with
h^{1}(L)=2
, extending the primary classification of Definition
1.10
(cf. Proposition
3.19
).

3.1.
No components for
4g-6\leq d\leq 4g-4

In the given range for
d
one can very easily prove the following:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
g\geq 4
,
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
and
4g-6\leq d\leq 4g-4
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Then
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

By Clifford’s Theorem (cf. e.g.,
[
8
, Theorem 2.1]
or
[
29
, Theorem 3]
), any rank-
2
(semi)stable bundle
\mathcal{F}
of degree
0\leq d\leq 4g-4
is such that
h^{0}(\mathcal{F})\leq\frac{d}{2}+2
. Thus, when
4g-5\leq d\leq 4g-4
, if we assume by contradiction
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)\neq\emptyset
then the general member
[\mathcal{F}]
of any irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
has speciality exactly
i=3
, as from Lemma
2.1
. Therefore,
h^{0}(\mathcal{F})=k_{3}=d-2g+5
; nevertheless, by Clifford’s Theorem above we should have
k_{3}=d-2g+5\leq\frac{d}{2}+2
which would give
d\leq 4g-6
, against the assumptions.

When otherwise
d=4g-6
, if once again by contradiction we had
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)\neq\emptyset
, then for
[\mathcal{F}]
general in any component we can apply
[
29
, Propositions 2,3,4]
; in any case, we would obtain
h^{0}(\mathcal{F})\leq\frac{d}{2}+1=2g-2
. However, as above, from Lemma
2.1
we would also get

h^{0}(\mathcal{F})=k_{3}=2g-1
, once again a contradiction.
∎

3.2.
Preliminary reductions for
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-7

Reducing to
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-7
, from Remark
1.7
, any possible irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
must have
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B})\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-3d-18
and, by Lemma
2.1
, a general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
must have
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
. Furthermore, by Proposition
1.9
,
\mathcal{F}
can be either of
first
or of
second type
in the sense of Definition
1.10
.

Putting all things together, we find below some necessary conditions for a component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
to exist as in the following result, where Lemmas
2.3
and
2.4
play a key role.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
and let
\mathcal{\mathcal{F}}
be a rank-
2
stable bundle, of speciality
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, degree

2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-7
and fitting in an exact sequence (
1.17
) where
\delta:=\mbox{deg}(\omega_{C}(-D))
(i.e.
D\in C^{(2g-2-\delta)}
), with
\delta\leq 2g-2
, and
\mbox{deg}(N)=d-\delta
.

(1) Assume
\mathcal{F}
to be a bundle of first type (in the sense of Definition
1.10
). Then one of the following occurs:

(1-a)

\delta\leq 2g-2-2\nu
, or

(1-b)

\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}
, or

(1-c)

\delta\leq\frac{4g-12}{3}
.

(2) If otherwise
\mathcal{F}
is assumed to be of second type, then one of the following occurs:

(2-a)

2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-3
, or

(2-b)

2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4-\nu
, or

(2-c)

2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{7g-6}{2}
.

Proof.

Consider the isomorphism
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)\cong B^{3}_{d^{\prime}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d^{\prime})
induced by the
Serre duality map

\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{E}:=\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}
, where
d^{\prime}=4g-4-d
. By Serre duality,
h^{0}(\mathcal{E})=3
, and
\mathcal{E}
fits into the extension:

(3.1)

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{L}\to 0,

where
\mathcal{L}:=\omega_{C}\otimes N^{\vee}
. Stability of
\mathcal{E}
and the fact that
D\geq 0
imply
0\leq\mbox{deg}(D)<d^{\prime}/2
,
\delta^{\prime}:=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{L})>\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}
. These bounds translate to
g\leq\delta:=\mbox{deg}(\omega_{C}(-D))\leq 2g-2
. We distinguish two cases:

(1) First Type (
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C}(D))=3
): since
\mbox{deg}(D)\leq g-2
,
\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)
must belong to an irreducible component of
W^{2}_{\mbox{deg}(D)}(C)
as classified in Lemma
2.4
. The non-emptiness criteria for these components yield the existence conditions:

(1-a)

\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}
implies
\mbox{deg}(D)\geq 2\nu
; this occurs iff
2g-2-\delta\geq 2\nu
.

(1-b)

\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
implies
2\mbox{deg}(D)-g-\nu-2\geq 0
. This occurs iff
3g-6-2\delta-\nu\geq 0
.

(1-c)

\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}
forces
3\mbox{deg}(D)-2g-6\geq 0
which holds iff
4g-12-3\delta\geq 0
.

(2) Second Type (
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C}(D))=1
): for
h^{0}(\mathcal{E})\geq 3
to hold, we must have
h^{0}(\mathcal{L})\geq 2
. Applying Lemma
2.3
to
\mathcal{L}\in\text{Pic}^{\delta^{\prime}}(C)
:

(2-a)

If
\mathcal{L}
is general, then
\delta^{\prime}\geq g+1
, which implies
d\leq 3g-5
.

(2-b)

If
\mathcal{L}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}
, non-emptiness requires
\delta^{\prime}\geq\nu
, thus
\nu\leq 4g-4-d
.

(2-c)

If
\mathcal{L}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}
, we require
\delta^{\prime}\geq\frac{g+2}{2}
, implying
4g-4-d\geq\frac{g+2}{2}
.

These cases provide the necessary conditions for the existence of components of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
.
∎

According to cases in Lemma
3.2
, we set the following:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
be an irreducible component.

(1) Assume its general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
is a bundle of first type in the sense of Definition
1.10
. Then, we will set
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(1-x)}
(respectively,
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,(1-x)}
) according to the fact that the component is superabundant (respectively, regular) and its general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a stable, rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
arising as in case (1-x), for
x=a,b,c
as in the proof of Lemma
3.2
, whose cases arise from Lemma
2.4
;

(2) Similarly, assume its general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
is a bundle of second type. Then, we will set
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(2-x)}
(respectively,
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,(2-x)}
) according to the fact that the component is superabundant (respectively, regular) and its general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a stable, rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
arising as in case (2-x), for
x=a,b,c
as in the proof of Lemma
3.2
, whose cases arise from Lemma
2.3
;

(3) If otherwise
\mathcal{B}
has been constructed via parametric approach using line-bundle extensions obtained by suitable natural modifications of case (1-x) (respectively, (2-x)), for
x=a,b,c
as above, then we will set

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(j-x)-mod}
(respectively,
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,(j-x)-mod}
) according to the fact that the component is superabundant (respectively, regular) and its general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a stable, rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
arising via
modification of case (j-x), for
j=1,2
and
x=a,b,c
as in the proof of Lemma
3.2
.

The following auxiliary result deals with rank-
2
vector bundles of
second type
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
and let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle of speciality
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is assumed to be of second type in the sense of Definition
1.10
. Let
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
be an effective divisor of degree
s>0

and
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+s}(C)
such that
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form (
1.17
).

(i) if
\mathcal{F}
is of type
(2-b)
as in Lemma
3.2
, assume that
N=K_{C}-(A+B_{b})
, where
A
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
,
B_{b}
is an effective divisor of degree
b\geq 0
such that
|A+B_{b}|=g^{1}_{\nu+b}
, i.e.
B_{b}
is the base locus for such a
g^{1}_{\nu+b}
, which is assumed to be such that
B_{b}\cap D_{s}=\emptyset
;

(ii) if
\mathcal{F}
is of type
(2-c)
as in Lemma
3.2
, assume that
N=K_{C}-g^{1}_{t}-B_{b}
, where
g^{1}_{t}
is a base point free linear series of degree
t
and dimension
1
,
B_{b}
is an effective divisor of degree
b\geq 0
such that
|g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}|=g^{1}_{t+b}
, i.e.
B_{b}
is the base locus for such a
g^{1}_{t+b}
, which is assumed to be such that
B_{b}\cap D_{s}=\emptyset
.

Then
\mathcal{F}
fits also in the exact sequence
0\to N(-D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}
is assumed to be of speciality
3
, of
second type
and fitting in an exact sequence of the form (
1.17
), one has
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D_{s}))=1
, i.e.
h^{0}(\omega_{C}(-D_{s}))=g-s
. Moreover
h^{1}(N)\geq 2
and

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
, for some
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-D_{s},N)
, i.e. one has

(u):\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}\to\omega_{C}(-D_{s})\to 0.

We may compose the line-bundle injection
N(-D_{s})\hookrightarrow N
to deduce that
N(-D_{s})
is also a sub-line bundle of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
; to prove the statement, we must show that this sub-line bundle is saturated in
\mathcal{F}
, i.e. that the zero-loci of the global sections of
\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee}
have empty intersection. This requires
h^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee}(-q))=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}(q))\leq 2
for every
q\in C
. Tensoring the dualized extension by
\omega_{C}\otimes N^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(D_{s})
yields:

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(D_{s})\xrightarrow{\sigma}\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee}\to\omega_{C}\otimes N^{\vee}\to 0.

The section
\tilde{\sigma}
induced by
\sigma
is non-vanishing for
q\notin D_{s}
. For
q\in D_{s}
, we tensor the original extension by
\mathcal{O}_{C}(q)
to obtain:

(3.2)

0\to N(q)\to\mathcal{F}_{u}(q)\to\omega_{C}-(D_{s}-q)\to 0.

In both cases (i) and (ii), the assumption
D_{s}\cap B_{b}=\emptyset
ensures that
h^{1}(N(q))=1
for any
q\in D_{s}
. Since
h^{1}(\omega_{C}-(D_{s}-q))=1
, the long exact sequence in cohomology associated to (
3.2
) implies
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}(q))\leq 1+1=2
. Thus, the intersection of zero-loci is empty, confirming that
N(-D_{s})
is saturated.
∎

3.3.
Some obstructions to fill-up components

As we will see below, Lemma
3.2
provides, for some values of
d
, explicit obstructions for any irreducible component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
to exist. Precisely we have the following situation.

\boxed{4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7:\;\mbox{no component}}
In this range for
d
, one has:

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
g\geq 15
,
3\leq\nu<\frac{g-2}{4}
and
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Then
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

By the chosen bounds on
\nu
, any potential irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
must superabundant, as the expected dimension
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
is strictly negative. We proceed by contradiction, assuming
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)\neq\emptyset
.

In the range
4g-3-\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
, cases
(2-a),\,(2-b)
, and
(2-c)
of Lemma
3.2
are numerically excluded. Consequently,
\mathcal{F}
must be of first type. However, analysis of cases
(1-a)
,
(1-b)
, and
(1-c)
reveals that the non-emptiness conditions for the required components of
W^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}(C)
conflict with the stability requirement
d-\delta\leq 2g-4
and the degree bounds on
d
and
\nu
. Thus, the locus is empty.

Arguments are similar for the range
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-4-\nu
; first-type bundles and cases
(2-a)
and
(2-c)
are excluded by the bounds on
\nu
and
d
. We then focus on case
(2-b)
, where
\mathcal{F}
is of second type and
K_{C}-N\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}
. Thus,
\mathcal{F}
fits into:

(3.3)

0\to K_{C}-(A+B_{b})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-D_{s}\to 0,

with
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
. By Lemma
3.4
-(i),
\mathcal{F}
also admits the extension
0\to K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}\to 0
. This implies the existence of a linear pencil of sections
|\mathcal{I}_{q/F}(\Gamma)|
on the surface scroll
F=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. By Proposition
1.4
, this pencil contains a reducible unisecant, identifying a sub-line bundle
K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f})
with
f=\mbox{deg}(E_{f})\geq\nu
. However, stability of
\mathcal{F}
in this degree range requires
d<4g-4-2f
, which implies
f<\nu
. This contradiction excludes case
(2-b)
, completing the proof.
∎

\boxed{2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu:\;\mbox{further obstructions and case exclusions}}
From Propositions
3.1
and
3.5
, we can therefore limit to the range:

(3.4)

2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu.

Remark 3.6
.

For
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
, the relevant range of
d
as defined in (
3.4
) is subdivided into two distinct intervals based on the sign of the Brill-Noether number
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
.

Superabundant Interval
: when
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}<0
any existing irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
necessarily is
superabundant
, i.e. of dimension higher than the expected one. This occurs when
\frac{10}{3}g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
, since
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
;

Regular/Mixed Interval
: when otherwise
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}\geq 0
, one may theoretically expect both
regular
components (where the dimension equals
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
) and
superabundant
ones; this occurs when
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
.

Therefore, the main subdivision that will be used below in our analysis is:

(3.5)

\frac{10}{3}g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu,\;{\rm with}\;3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3},\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6.

This serves as a primary framework for our analysis; the subsequent results identify further obstructions, effectively narrowing the candidate cases initially presented in Lemma
3.2
.

\boxed{\mbox{Obstructions: Case $(1-c)$ can never give rise to a component}}
Concerning bundles of type
(1-c)
as in Lemma
3.2
, one has the following result.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Let
\mathcal{B}\subset B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
be an irreducible locus, whose general element
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
is assumed to be a stable, rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
and fitting in an exact sequence of the form

(u):\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-E\to 0,

where
E\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}\subseteq W^{2}_{t}(C)
for some integer
t
, whereas
N\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+t}(C)
. Then
\mathcal{B}
cannot be an irreducible component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
.

In particular, case
(1-c)
as in Lemma
3.2
can never give rise to an irreducible component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
.

Proof.

Since
h^{1}(K_{C}-E)=h^{0}(E)=3=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})
, the extension
(u)
implies that either
N
is non-special or the coboundary map
\partial_{u}:H^{0}(K_{C}-E)\to H^{1}(N)
is surjective. From Lemma
2.4
,
E
must belong to a component such as
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}
, hence
t\geq\frac{2g}{3}+2
and
\operatorname{dim}(\{E\})\leq 3t-2g-6
. Stability of
\mathcal{F}
requires
d<4g-4-2t
. This bound implies
\mbox{deg}(2K_{C}-N-E)>2g-2
; thus, by Riemann-Roch,
\operatorname{dim}(\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-E,N))=5g-5-d-2t
. Summing the dimensions of the choices for
N
,
E
, and the projectivized extension space
\mathbb{P}(\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-E,N)
we obtain:

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B})\leq g+(3t-2g-6)+(5g-6-d-2t)=4g-12-d+t.

For
\mathcal{B}
to be a component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, it must satisfy
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B})\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-3d-18
. This requirement leads to the inequality:

(4g-12-d+t)-(10g-3d-18)=2d+6-6g+t\geq 0\implies d\geq 3g-3-\frac{t}{2}.

However, combining this with the stability bound
d<4g-4-2t
yields:

3g-3-\frac{t}{2}\leq d<4g-4-2t\implies g-1<\frac{3t}{2}.

Substituting the non-emptiness condition
t\geq\frac{2g}{3}+2
gives
(4g-4-2t)-(3g-3-\frac{t}{2})=g-1-\frac{3t}{2}\leq-4<0
. This contradiction ensures that case
(1\text{-}c)
of Lemma
3.2
cannot yield a component.
∎

\boxed{\mbox{Obstructions: further case exclusions for $d$ near the upper-bound in \eqref{eq:29dicnewbis}}}
The following result shows that there are some extra case exclusions for a component to exist.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. One has the following:

(i)
For any
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and

\frac{10}{3}g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
, there is no component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
arising from extensions as in cases
(1-b)
and
(2-a)
in Lemma
3.2
.

(ii)
For any
g\geq 17
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-5}{4}
and

\frac{7g-5}{2}\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
, there is no component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
arising from extensions as in case
(2-c)
in Lemma
3.2
.

(iii)
For any integers
0\leq s\leq\frac{g}{2}
,

3g-5\leq d\leq\frac{7}{2}g-5-s\leq\frac{7}{2}g-5
, there is no stable bundle
\mathcal{F}
arising from an extension as in case
(2-c)
in Lemma
3.2
of the form

(3.6)

0\to N:=K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-D_{s}\to 0,

where
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
is general whereas
t+b=4g-4-d-s
,
g^{1}_{t}
is a base point free linear system and
B_{b}\geq 0
(possibly
B_{b}=0
) is an effective divisor of base points, i.e.
g^{1}_{t+b}=|g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}|=g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}
(in other words

K_{C}-N=g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}\subseteq W^{1}_{4g-4-d-s}(C)
, as in Lemma
3.2
). In particular, there is no component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]
is a bundle as in the previous description.

Proof.

(i)
and
(ii)
: Cases
(1-b)
,
(2-a)
, and
(2-c)
of Lemma
3.2
are numerically excluded in the range
d\geq\frac{10}{3}g-5
. For instance, in
(1-b)
, stability requires
d<2\delta\leq 3g-6-\nu
, which is strictly less than the given lower bound for
d
.

(iii)
To analyze the remaining cases, we employ the following:

Claim 3.9
.

Let
C
be a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 4
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-
2
vector bundle on
C
of degree
d=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})\geq 3g-5
and of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
. Assume that
\mathcal{F}
fits in

(u):\;\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-D_{s}\to 0
where:

(i)

h^{0}(K_{C}-N)=2
,

(ii)

D_{s}
is an effective divisor such that
h^{0}(D_{s})=1
,

(iii)

\mbox{deg}(N)\leq\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-D_{s})
.

Then either
{\rm Bs}(|K_{C}-N|)\neq\emptyset
or
