h^{0}(N-D_{s})\geq h^{0}(N)-s+1
.

Proof of Claim
3.9
.

If
\text{Bs}(|K_{C}-N|)=\emptyset
and
h^{0}(N-D_{s})=h^{0}(N)-s
, the base-point-free pencil trick implies
\operatorname{dim}(\text{im}(\mu))=3g-3-\mbox{deg}(N)-s
. However, for
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, the coboundary map must vanish, implying
\operatorname{dim}(\text{im}(\mu))<\operatorname{dim}(\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-D_{s},N))
. Riemann-Roch shows these two dimensions are equal, yielding a contradiction.
∎

For bundles
\mathcal{F}
as in (
3.6
), we verify that the assumptions of Claim
3.9
are satisfied. Specifically, since
N=K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})
, we have
h^{0}(K_{C}-N)=2
. For a general
D_{s}
of degree
s\leq g/2
, one has
h^{0}(D_{s})=1
. Condition
\mbox{deg}(N)\leq\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-N)
is satisfied for the given range of
d
. Generality of
D_{s}
implies
h^{0}(N-D_{s})=h^{0}(N)-s
. As a consequence of Claim
3.9
, the linear system
|K_{C}-N|
must have a non-empty base locus, meaning
B_{b}
is an effective divisor of degree
b>0
. This effectively proves part
(iii)
of Proposition
3.8
for the case where
B_{b}=0
(i.e., when
K_{C}-N
is general in
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}
). Moving to the case
B_{b}\neq 0
, the disjointness of
B_{b}
and
D_{s}
allows us to apply Lemma
3.4
-(ii), showing that
\mathcal{F}
fits into the following exact sequence:

(3.7)

0\to K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}+D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}\to 0.

This representation is fundamental for applying Claim
3.10
below, which allows one to find bounds on the Segre invariant
s(\mathcal{F})
.

Claim 3.10
.

Let
C
be any smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 4
and let
g^{1}_{t}
be a base point free complete pencil on
C
. Assume that a rank-
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
on
C
, with
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, fits into an exact sequence of the form:

(3.8)

0\to K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0,

where
B_{b}
is an effective divisor of degree
b
with
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})=2.
Then
s(\mathcal{F})\leq-t+b
.

Proof of Claim
3.10
.

Let
F=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
and let
\widetilde{\Gamma}
be the canonical section corresponding to the surjection
\mathcal{F}\twoheadrightarrow\omega_{C}
in (
3.8
). Given
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, we have
h^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})=3
, hence
\operatorname{dim}|\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})|=2
. For a general point
q\in F
projecting to
p\in C
, the pencil
|\mathcal{I}_{q/F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})|
contains a reducible unisecant
\Gamma=\Gamma_{D_{p}}+f_{D_{p}}
, where
\Gamma_{D_{p}}
is a section and
D_{p}\geq p
. This section induces:

(3.9)

0\to K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})+D_{p}\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-D_{p}\to 0.

The existence of this section implies
h^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}(-D_{p}))\geq 1
. Tensoring the dual of (
3.8
) by
\omega_{C}(-D_{p})
, we obtain
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}-D_{p})\geq 1
. Since
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})=2
and
p
is general, we have
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}-D_{p})=1
. Dualizing (
3.9
) and tensoring by
\omega_{C}
yields
h^{0}(D_{p})=2
and
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}-D_{p})=1
, which implies
|D_{p}|=g^{1}_{t}+B^{\prime}
for some
0\leq B^{\prime}\leq B_{b}
. Thus, (
3.9
) rewrites as:

0\to K_{C}-(B_{b}-B^{\prime})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-g^{1}_{t}-B^{\prime}\to 0,

from which we compute
s(\mathcal{F})\leq\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-g^{1}_{t}-B^{\prime})-\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-B_{b}+B^{\prime})=-t+b
.
∎

Claim 3.11
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve, ad let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-
2
vector bundle of degree
d=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})\geq 3g-5
and of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
which is assumed to fit in an exact sequence of the form

(3.10)

0\to K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-D_{s}\to 0,

such that

(1)
g^{1}_{t}
is a base-point-free complete pencil of degree
t
;

(2)
B_{b}\in C^{(b)}
and
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
such that
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})=h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}+D_{s})=2
;

(3)
2t\geq t+b+s=4g-4-d
.

Then
\mathcal{F}
is not stable.

Proof of Claim
3.11
.

Let
B^{\prime}:=B_{b}\cap D_{s}
, where
0\leq\mbox{deg}(B^{\prime})=b^{\prime}
. Dualizing (
3.10
) and tensoring by
\omega_{C}
yields
H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\simeq H^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})\oplus H^{0}(D_{s})
since
h^{0}(D_{s})=1
. This implies that every section in
H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
vanishes on
B^{\prime}
, so
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime}))=3
. Tensoring (
3.10
) by
\mathcal{O}_{C}(B^{\prime})
gives:

(3.11)

0\to K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}-B^{\prime})\to\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime})\to K_{C}-(D_{s}-B^{\prime})\to 0.

By definition of
B^{\prime}
, the divisors
(D_{s}-B^{\prime})
and
(B_{b}-B^{\prime})
are disjoint. Since
g^{1}_{t}
is base-point-free, we apply Lemma
3.4
-(ii) to show
\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime})
also fits into:

(3.12)

0\to K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}+D_{s}-2B^{\prime})\to\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime})\to K_{C}\to 0.

By assumption (2),
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}+D_{s})=2
. Thus,
\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime})
satisfies assumptions in Claim
3.10
, yielding:

s(\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime}))\leq-t+(b-b^{\prime})+(s-b^{\prime})=-t+b+s-2b^{\prime}\leq-t+b+s.

Finally, assumption (3) (
2t\geq t+b+s
) implies
-t+b+s\leq 0
. Consequently,
s(\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime}))\leq 0
, proving that
\mathcal{F}(B^{\prime})
(and thus
\mathcal{F}
) is not stable.
∎

We may complete the proof of
(iii)
. Indeed, by assumptions,
\mathcal{F}
fits in (
3.6
) necessarily with
B_{b}>0
(as the case
B_{b}=0
has been already ruled-out after the proof of Claim
3.9
). Notice that the assumptions
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
general,
K_{C}-N=g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}\subseteq W^{1}_{4g-4-d-s}(C)
as in Lemma
3.2
and
0\leq s\leq\frac{g}{2}
,

3g-5\leq d\leq\frac{7}{2}g-5-s\leq\frac{7}{2}g-5
,
t+b+s=4g-4-d
imply that assumptions as in Claim
3.11
hold true. Indeed, (1) is obviously satisfied by
g^{1}_{t}
; as for (3), i.e.
2t\geq t+b+s=4g-4-d
, notice that from
d\geq 3g-5
we have
4g-4-d\leq g+1
whereas from
g^{1}_{t}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}\subseteq W^{1}_{t}(C)
and from Lemma
3.2
we have that
2t-g-2\geq 0
, i.e.
2t\geq g+2
.

Finally we have to check if assumption (2) is satisfied, i.e. we have to prove that
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}+D_{s})=2
. By Riemann-Roch

h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}+D_{s})=(t+b+s)-g+1+h^{0}(K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})-D_{s})
. Since

h^{0}(K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}))=h^{1}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})=g+1-t-b
, the generality of
D_{s}
gives

h^{0}(K_{C}-(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b})-D_{s})={\rm max}\{0,g+1-t-b-s\}
and, from the fact that
(
3.10
) gives
d=4g-4-t-b-s\geq 3g-5
, we get
g+1-t-b-s\geq 0
. Therefore, from above
h^{0}(g^{1}_{t}+B_{b}+D_{s})=(t+b+s)-g+1+(g+1-t-b-s)=2
. Thus, from Claim
3.11
,
\mathcal{F}
is not stable and the proof of
(iii)
is complete.
∎

\boxed{\mbox{Obstructions: further case exclusions for $d$ near the lower-bound in \eqref{eq:29dicnewbis}}}
The following constraints, valid for values of
d
close to the lower bound in (
3.5
), lead to additional case exclusions. In particular recall, from Proposition
3.7
, that case
(1-c)
in Lemma
3.2
can never yield an irreducible component; for this reason, it will be never mentioned in the statement of the next result.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
g\geq 7
,
3\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Assume that
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(3,d)\neq\emptyset
and let
\mathcal{B}
be any of its irreducible component, whose general point is denoted by
[\mathcal{F}]
. Then one of the following cases must occur.

(1) If
\mathcal{F}
is of first type, with
\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)
as in (
1.17
), then:

(1-a)

either
\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}\subseteq W^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}(C)
, where necessarily either
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
and
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{6}
, or
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
and
\frac{g}{6}<\nu<\frac{g}{2}
. In particular, case
(1-a)
cannot occur for
\frac{g}{6}<\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
4g-4-4\nu\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
;

(1-b)

or
\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subseteq W^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}(C)
, where necessarily
2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu<\frac{10}{3}g-6
for any
3\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
. In particular, for
3g-6-\nu\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
case
(1-b)
can never occur.

(2) If otherwise
\mathcal{F}
is of second type, then:

(2-a)

either
N
is general of its degree and either
g=7,8
, for any
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
and
3\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
or, when
g\geq 9
, necessarily
2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-3<\frac{10}{3}g-6
, for any
3\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
. In particular, for
g\geq 9
and
3g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, case
(2-a)
cannot occur;

(2-b)

or
N
is such that
K_{C}-N\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}\subseteq W^{1}_{2g-2+\delta-d}(C)
and either
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, for any
g\geq 12
and
3\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
, or
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, for
7\leq g\leq 11
and
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{2}{3}g-2
. In particular, for
7\leq g\leq 11
,
\frac{2}{3}g-1\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, case
(2-b)
cannot occur;

(2-c)

or finally
N
is such that
K_{C}-N\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}\subseteq W^{1}_{2g-2+\delta-d}(C)
, for any
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
and
3\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
.

Proof.

A general points
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
corresponds to a bundle of either first or second type, satisfying stability condition
d<2\delta
. We evaluate the numerical consistency of the cases from Lemma
3.2
within the range
2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
.

(1) Concerning first type bundles, we have:

(1-a)

If
\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,2}}}
, then
\delta\leq 2g-2-2\nu
, implying
d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
. This range covers the entire interval if
\nu\leq g/6
. For
g/6<\nu<g/2
, the case is restricted to
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
.

(1-b)

If
\mathcal{O}_{C}(D)\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
, then
\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}
, implying
d\leq 3g-7-\nu
. This sub-interval always lies within
[2g-2,\frac{10}{3}g-6]
.

(1-c)

This case is excluded by Proposition
3.7
.

(2) As for second type bundles, we have:

(2-a)

General quotients
\mathcal{L}
require
d\leq 3g-3
, which is strictly less than the upper bound
\frac{10}{3}g-6
for
g\geq 9
.

(2-b)

If
K_{C}-N\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}
, then
d\leq 4g-4-\nu
. For
g\geq 12
, this always holds. For
7\leq g\leq 11
, it requires
\nu\leq\frac{2}{3}g-2
.

(2-c)

If
K_{C}-N\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,0}}}
, non-emptiness requires
d\leq\frac{7g-6}{2}
. Since
\frac{7g-6}{2}<\frac{10}{3}g-6
for the given genus, this case is confined to the lower part of the degree range.

∎

3.4.
Components from bundles of first type

In the light of Propositions
3.8
–
(i)
and
3.12
–
(1)
, there are no irreducible components of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
whose general point corresponds to a bundle of
first type
as in case
(1\text{-}b)
of Lemma
3.2
, for
\frac{10}{3}g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
and, respectively, for
3g-6-\nu\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
. Similarly, for case
(1\text{-}c)
, the same conclusion holds true because of Proposition
3.7
. Therefore, for
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-7
and among bundles of
first type
, case
(1\text{-}a)
as in Lemma
3.2
remains the only admissible candidate for yielding an irreducible component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
filled by bundles of
first type
. For this reason, in this section we focus on Case
(1\text{-}a)
.

\boxed{2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu:\;\mbox{Components from Case (1-a)}}
In the range
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-7
, we have the following:

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve
C
of genus
g
.

(i)
If
g\geq 4
,
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
and
4g-4-4\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
, there is no irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is of first type as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
3.2
.

(ii)
If
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}(B_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)})=6g-6-4\nu-d.

In particular
it is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(1-a)}
, when
d\geq 2g-5+2\nu
whereas it is regular, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,\,3,\,(1-a)}
, when otherwise
d=2g-6+2\nu
.

Furthermore, its general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in B_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
, speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is of first type as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
3.2
and fitting in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
, whereas
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
is general and
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
is the only irreducible component whose general point arises from a bundle
\mathcal{F}
of first type on
C
.

(iii)
If
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
2g-2\leq d\leq 2g-7+2\nu
, there is no component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is of first type as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
3.2
.

Proof.

(i). For a component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
to contain stable bundles, the stability condition requires
d<2\delta
, where
\delta=\mbox{deg}(L)
for any quotient line bundle
L
. For a bundle of first type (case 1-a), the quotient
L=\omega_{C}(-D)
satisfies
h^{1}(L)=3
. According to Lemma
2.4
, this needs
\delta\leq 2g-2-2\nu
. Combining these yields the sharp existence bound:

d<2\delta\leq 4g-4-4\nu.

(ii). We construct the component by considering the extension:

(u):0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0

where
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
is the unique
\nu
-gonal pencil. We set the quotient
L=K_{C}-2A
, so
\delta=2g-2-2\nu
. By Proposition
1.1
,
h^{0}(2A)=3
(since
g\geq 2\nu-2
), which via Serre duality implies
h^{1}(L)=3
. This confirms that
\mathcal{F}_{u}
has the required speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})\geq h^{1}(L)=3
. The condition
h^{0}(L)=g+2-2\nu>0
ensures the existence of the quotient line bundle.

Claim 3.14
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve with
d
and
\nu
as in the above assumptions. Let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be a rank-
2
vector bundle arising as a general extension
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,N)
, where
N
is a general line bundle in
{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
. Then
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable and such that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=h^{1}(K_{C}-2A)=3
.

Proof of Claim
3.14
.

Set the quotient line bundle
L:=K_{C}-2A
with
\mbox{deg}(L)=\delta=2g-2-2\nu
. For a general line bundle
N
of degree
d-\delta
, numerical assumption
d<4g-4-4\nu
ensures that
\delta>\frac{d}{2}
and
N\ncong L
. The dimension of the extension space is:

m:=\operatorname{dim}(\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=5g-5-4\nu-d>0.

We may verify
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
by analyzing the coboundary map
\partial_{u}:H^{0}(L)\to H^{1}(N)
. Let
\ell=h^{0}(L)=g+2-2\nu
and
r=h^{1}(N)
. Whether
N
is special or non-special, the condition
\ell\geq r
holds for
d\geq 2g-2
. By Theorem
2.2
, for a general
u\in\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
,
\partial_{u}
is surjective, implying
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=h^{1}(L)=3
.

To prove that the general extension
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable (
s(\mathcal{F}_{u})>0
), we distinguish two cases based on the difference
2\delta-d
:

\bullet
If
2\delta-d\geq 2
, i.e.
d\leqslant 4g-6-4\nu
, we may apply Proposition
1.11
, with
X=\varphi(C)
the image in
\mathbb{P}:=\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A\color[rgb]{0,0,0},N))
via the morphism
\varphi=\varphi_{|2K_{C}-2A-N|}
. From above one has
m=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A\color[rgb]{0,0,0},N))=5g-5-4\nu-d
so, for any integer
\sigma\leq g
, one has

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}((4g-4-4\nu-d)+\sigma-2)}(X)\right)<m-1=\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}),

so
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable in this case.

\bullet
when otherwise
2\delta-d=1
, hence
d=4g-5-4\nu
(odd), if
\mathcal{F}_{u}
were unstable, it would admit a sub-line bundle
\mathcal{M}
with
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})\geq\frac{d+1}{2}
. Since
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})>\mbox{deg}(N)
, the only possible map
\mathcal{M}\to L
is an isomorphism. This would imply the extension splits, which contradicts the choice of a general
u\neq 0
in the
g
-dimensional extension space. Thus, also in this case
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable.
∎

From previous arguments, one can consider a vector bundle

\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu}
on a suitable open, dense subset
S\subseteq\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu+b}(C)
, described by
N
varying in
S
, whose rank is

m=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,\,N))=5g-5-d-4\nu
. Taking the associated projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,b})\to S
(consisting of
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,\;N)\right)
’s as
N
varies in
S
) one has
\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu}))=g+(5g-6-d-4\nu)=6g-6-d-4\nu
. The latter quantity equals
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
only when
d=2g-6+2\nu
, whereas it is bigger than
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
for
d>2g-6+2\nu
. When otherwise
d<2g-6+2\nu
, the dimension of
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu})
is instead less than
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
(which in particular will also prove
(iii)
). From Claim
3.14
, one moreover has a natural (rational) modular map

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{d,\nu}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}

\displaystyle U_{C}(d)

\displaystyle(N,[u])\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u};

such that
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
.

Claim 3.15
.

In the above assumptions, the map
\pi_{d,\nu}
is birational onto its image. In particular,
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
is uniruled and one has also that

\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu}))=\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu}))=6g-6-d-4\nu
.

Proof of Claim
3.15
.

The image
\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
is uniruled as it is dominated by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu})
, which is ruled over
S
. To determine its dimension, we show that the map
\pi_{d,\nu}
is birational onto its image.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be a general bundle in
\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
. Suppose there exists a distinct pre-image
(N^{\prime},[u^{\prime}])
such that
\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{u}
. Since the quotient
L=K_{C}-2A
is fixed, determinantal considerations force
N^{\prime}\cong N
. If
[u]\neq[u^{\prime}]
, one gets the presence of an isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{F}_{u}
ov vector bundles which would provide two linearly independent sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})
. We will show instead that
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})=1
.

By Serre duality,
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes 2})
. Tensoring the extension
(u)
by
A^{\otimes 2}
yields:

0\to N\otimes A^{\otimes 2}\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes 2}\to K_{C}\to 0.

\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes 2}
corresponds to a general element in
\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C},N\otimes A^{\otimes 2})
. We distinguish two cases:

\bullet
if
h^{1}(N\otimes A^{\otimes 2})=0
, then
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes 2})=h^{1}(K_{C})=1
;

\bullet
if otherwise
h^{1}(N\otimes A^{\otimes 2})\geq 1
, the generality of
N
and the numerical bounds on
d
ensure the hypotheses of Theorem
2.2
are met. Thus, the coboundary map
H^{0}(K_{C})\to H^{1}(N\otimes A^{\otimes 2})
is surjective, again yielding
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes 2})=h^{1}(K_{C})=1
.

In both cases,
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})=1
, implying
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are linearly dependent hence
[u]=[u^{\prime}]
. Thus,
\pi_{d,\nu}
is birational and
\operatorname{dim}(\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu}))=6g-6-d-4\nu
.
∎

Let us denote by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}
the closure of
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
in
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
; from Claim
3.15
, one has

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)})=6g-6-d-4\nu,

since
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
is dense in it. We want to find under which conditions
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}
gives rise to an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
and, if so, determine when it is also generically smooth. To do so, by Claim
3.14
, we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(K_{C}-2A)=h^{0}(2A)=3
; consider the following diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccc}&&0&&0&&0&&\\[3.44444pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[3.44444pt]
0&\rightarrow&N+2A-K_{C}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\otimes 2}\otimes\omega^{\vee}_{C}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{C}&\rightarrow&0\\[3.44444pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&\\[3.44444pt]
0&\rightarrow&N\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{(}K_{C}-2A)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}&\rightarrow&0\\[3.44444pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[3.44444pt]
0&\rightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{C}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}\otimes N^{\vee}&\rightarrow&(K_{C}-2A)\otimes N^{\vee}&0&\\[3.44444pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[3.44444pt]
&&0&&0&&0&&\end{array}

which arises from the exact sequence
0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0
tensored by its dual sequence. If we tensor the column in the middle of the above diagram by
\omega_{C}
, we get
H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\otimes 2})\hookrightarrow H^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
. From the isomorphism
H^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\simeq H^{0}(2A)
, the Petri map of
\mathcal{F}
reads

\mu_{\mathcal{F}}:H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(2A)\to H^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}).

Moreover, from the same column tensored by
\omega_{C}
, we get also the injection

(3.13)

H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes 2A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{h}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}H^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})

from which we deduce that the kernel of
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
is the same as the kernel of the following multiplication map

H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(2A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{F},\;2A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes 2A).

We need to investigate the connection between the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
and
the multiplication map
\mu_{\mathcal{F},\;2A}
.

Let us therefore consider

[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}
general, so that
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
fits in an exact sequence of the form

(3.14)

(u)\ \ \ 0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0,

in which
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g-2-2\nu}(C)
is general. Dualizing (
3.14
), one gets

(3.15)

0\to 2A\to K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee}\to K_{C}-N\to 0,

whence one has the natural injection

H^{0}(2A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\iota}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}H^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee})
which is indeed an isomorphism as

h^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=h^{0}(2A)=3
. Taking into account the injection (
3.13
), in sum we obtain the following commutative diagram where, to ease notation, we have set
\mu^{\prime}:=\mu_{\mathcal{F},\;2A}
:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccc}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee})&&\\[3.44444pt]
\uparrow{\rm Id}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\iota}}&&\uparrow\mathfrak{h}&\\[3.44444pt]
H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(2A)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A)&\end{array}

We notice that
{\rm Id}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\iota}}
is an isomorphism so, by the injectivity of
\mathfrak{h}
, it follows that

(3.16)

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}({\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u}}}))=\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu^{\prime})).

We focus on the computation of
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu^{\prime})).
To do this, we establish the following more general result.

Claim 3.16
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve and let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be a rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle arising as a general extension as in (
3.14
), where
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
is general. If
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
, then for any integer
1\leq k\leq 2
the multiplication map

\mu_{k}:H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes(k-1)})\otimes H^{0}(A)\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes k})

is surjective.

Proof of Claim
3.16
.

To show the surjectivity of the multiplication maps
\mu_{k}
, we view
\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes s}
as a general element of
\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(2-s)A,N+sA)
for
-1\leq s\leq 2
.First, we verify the hypotheses of Theorem
2.2
. Let
\ell=h^{0}(K_{C}-(2-s)A)
and
r=h^{1}(N+sA)
. Since
3\nu\leq g
, Proposition
1.1
implies
\ell=g-(2-s)\nu+2-s
. Given
d\geq 2g+2\nu-6
and the generality of
N
, a dimension count confirms
\ell\geq r
. Furthermore, the dimension of the extension space
m=5g-5-4\nu-d
satisfies
m\geq\ell+1
within the prescribed range for
d
. Thus, by Theorem
2.2
, the coboundary maps
\partial_{u}:H^{0}(K_{C}-(2-s)A)\to H^{1}(N+sA)
are surjective for all
-1\leq s\leq 2
. This surjectivity implies
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes s})=h^{1}(K_{C}-(2-s)A)
, which equals
3-s
by Proposition
1.1
. Riemann-Roch then provides:

(3.17)

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes s})=d+2s\nu-2g-s+5.

For
1\leq k\leq 2
, consider the sequence derived from the base-point-free-pencil trick on
A
:

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes(k-2)})\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes(k-1)})\otimes H^{0}(A)\xrightarrow{\mu_{k}}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes k}).

From this,
\operatorname{dim}(\text{Im }\mu_{k})=2h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes(k-1)})-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes(k-2)})
. Substituting the values from (
3.17
), we find that
\operatorname{dim}(\text{Im }\mu_{k})
exactly matches
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\otimes k})
, thereby proving that
\mu_{k}
is surjective.
∎

To show that the multiplication map
\mu^{\prime}:H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(2A)\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A)
is surjective, we use Claim
3.16
through the following iterative argument. Since
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are surjective, the composed map
\tilde{\mu}:=\mu_{2}\circ(\mu_{1}\otimes\text{Id}_{H^{0}(A)})
is also surjective:

\tilde{\mu}:H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(A)^{\otimes 2}\twoheadrightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A).

We use the fact that the multiplication map of the pencil
\mu_{2A}:H^{0}(A)^{\otimes 2}\to H^{0}(2A)
is surjective (as
\operatorname{dim}|2A|=2
). This leads to the following diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(A)^{\otimes 2}&\xrightarrow{\text{Id}\otimes\mu_{2A}}&H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(2A)\\
\downarrow\tilde{\mu}&&\downarrow\mu^{\prime}\\
H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A)&=&H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A)\end{array}

Since both
\tilde{\mu}
and
(\text{Id}\otimes\mu_{2A})
are surjections, a diagram-chase confirms that
\mu^{\prime}
must also be surjective.

Claim 3.17
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve and let
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
be an integer. The locus
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}
constructed above is a reduced, irreducible component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
which is uniruled, generically smooth and of dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)})=6g-6-d-4\nu
.

Furthermore, if
d=2g-6+2\nu
,
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,(1-a)}
, i.e. it is a regular component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(2,d)
; if otherwise
2g-5+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
,
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(1-a)}
is a superabundant component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(2,d)
.

Proof of Claim
3.17
.

The locus
\mathcal{B}_{3,(1\text{-}a)}
is uniruled, as it is the closure of
\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
, which is dominated by the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu})
. By Claim
3.15
, its dimension is:

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{3,(1\text{-}a)})=6g-6-d-4\nu.

To prove generic smoothness, we compute the dimension of the tangent space at a general point
[\mathcal{F}_{u}]
. Since
\mu^{\prime}
is surjective,
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u}})=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})h^{0}(2A)-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A)
. Recalling that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=h^{0}(2A)=3
, the Petri map dimension formula gives:

\displaystyle\operatorname{dim}T_{[\mathcal{F}_{u}]}(\mathcal{B}_{3,(1\text{-}a)})

\displaystyle=4g-3-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})h^{1}(\mathcal{F}u)+\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}\mu{\mathcal{F}_{u}})\&=4g-3-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A).

Replacing
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes 2A)=d+4\nu-2g+3
from (
3.17
), we obtain exactly
6g-6-d-4\nu
, confirming that the component is generically smooth.

Finally, the component is superabundant because
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{3,(1\text{-}a)})\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
whenever
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
, with equality (regularity) holding iff
d=2g-6+2\nu
.
∎

To conclude the proof of
(ii)
, we establish the uniqueness of the irreducible component
\mathcal{B}_{3,(1-a)}
by excluding alternative bundle types from Lemma
3.2
.
Following Proposition
3.7
, bundles of type
(1-c)
do not fill-up an irreducible component for any degree
d
. Similalry bundles of type
(1-b)
cannot fill-up
an irreducible component in the range
3g-6-\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
from Propositions
3.8
-
(i)
and
3.12
-
(1)
; such bundles are later shown (cf. Lemma
3.26
and Proposition
3.30
below) to be also incapable of forming a component in the lower range
2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu
.

Thus,
\mathcal{B}_{3,(1-a)}
remains the only candidate. We now show that within bundles of type
(1-a)
, the specific construction using the
\nu
-gonal pencil is the unique way to obtain a component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
. This will be proved in the following:

Claim 3.18
.

Let
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve. Assume that
\mathcal{B}
is any irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
arising as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
3.2
. Then
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{3,(1-a)}
as in Claim
3.17
.

Proof of Claim
3.18
.

Since, by assumption,
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
general arises as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
3.2
, we have

K_{C}-L\cong A+B_{b}
, where
B_{b}\in C^{(b)}
is the base locus of
|A+B_{b}|
of degree
b\geq 0
. Moreover
\mathcal{F}
corresponds to a suitable
v\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-B_{b},N_{b})
, for some line bundle
N_{b}\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+b}(C)
. Moreover, always by assumption,
L=K_{C}-A-B_{b}
is such that
h^{1}(L)=h^{1}(K_{C}-A-B_{b})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
; therefore, by taking cohomology in

0\to N_{b}\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-A-B_{b}\to 0,

regardless the speciality of
N_{b}
, the corresponding
coboundary map
H^{0}(K_{C}-A-B_{b})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{v}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(N_{b})
has to be surjective. From semicontinuity on
{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-B_{b},N_{b})
and the fact that semistability is an open condition in irreducible flat families, for a general
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-B_{b},N_{b})
the coboundary map
\partial_{u}
is also surjective and
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is semistable, of speciality
3
.

Since
\mathcal{B}
is an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
and since

u
general specializes to
v\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-B_{b},N_{b})
, then

[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
general has to come from a general
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-B_{b},N_{b})
, for some
B_{b}\in C^{(b)}
and from some
N_{b}\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+b}(C)
. On the other hand one observes that a general extension as

(*):\;\;\;0\to N_{b}\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-A-B_{b}\to 0

is a
flat specialization
of a general extension of the form

(**):\;\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-A\to 0,

where
N\cong N_{b}-B_{b}
. To observe this, it suffices to note that extensions of type
(**)
are parametrized by the vector space
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)\cong H^{1}(N+A-K_{C})
, while extensions
(*)
are parametrized by
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-B_{b},N+B_{b})\cong H^{1}(N+2B_{b}+A-K_{C})
. The existence of such a flat specialization is ensured by the surjectivity

H^{1}(N+A-K_{C})\twoheadrightarrow H^{1}(N+2B_{b}+A-K_{C}),

which follows from the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(2B_{b})\to\mathcal{O}_{2B_{b}}\to 0
tensored by
N+A-K_{C}
. For an interpretation of this surjectivity via
elementary transformations
of vector bundles, see
[
24
, pp. 101-102]
.

From Claim
3.17
, the construction of
\mathcal{B}_{3,(1-a)}
and semicontinuity, it follows that general extensions
(**)
give rise to points in

\mathcal{B}_{3,(1-a)}
. By flat specialization of a general extension
(**)
to a general extension
(*)
, one can conclude that
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{3,(1-a)}
, i.e. that
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{3,(1-a)}
.
∎

The above arguments conclude the proof of
(ii)
.

(iii).
From computations as in
(ii)
, recall that when
d<2g-6+2\nu
the dimension of the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu})
is smaller than
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
, namely condition
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
is necessary for the existence of an irreducible component. Therefore also
(iii)
is proved.
∎

3.5.
Components from modifications of bundles of second type

As for vector bundles of
second type
, Propositions
3.8
and
3.12

rule out the existence of irreducible components of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
whose general point corresponds to a bundle as in case
(2\text{-}a)
of Lemma
3.2
,
for
3g-2\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
. Similarly, Proposition
3.8
implies the same conclusion for case
(2\text{-}c)
when
\frac{7g-5}{2}\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
.

Therefore, within
\frac{7g-5}{2}\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
, case
(2\text{-}b)
in Lemma
3.2
remains the only potential candidate giving rise to possible irreducible components of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
filled-up by bundles of
second type
.
However, in Proposition
3.19
–
(i)
below, we show that if
3g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
, and a vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
of type
(2\text{-}b)
is to
represent the general point of such a component, the kernel line bundle
N
in its presentation — satisfying
\omega_{C}\otimes N^{\vee}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}{1,1}}}\subseteq W^{1}_{\nu+b}(C)
as required in case
(2\text{-}b)
— must be such that
{\rm Bs}(|\omega_{C}\otimes N^{\vee}|)\neq\emptyset
(recall description of the elements in the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{1,1}}}
as in Lemma
2.3
-
(i)
). Nevertheless, we will also show that any such bundle admits an
alternative presentation
without base locus, namely via the quotient line bundle
\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}
,
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
(see the proof of Claim
3.20
). In this setting the line bundle
\omega_{C}(-D)
, where
D
general effective, which is expected to serve as the quotient line bundle in a presentation of a bundle
\mathcal{F}
of type
(2\text{-}b)
, can be replaced in the alternative presentation by the quotient
\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}
and appears instead as the
kernel
line bundle of the alternative presentation.

Although this alternative presentation does not fall under the classification of either
first
or
second type
bundles as in Definition
1.10
, it considerably simplifies both the explicit construction of desired components and the analysis of their Zariski tangent spaces (see e.g. Proposition
3.19
). Moreover, as an additional benefit, this approach extends to lower values of
d
, specifically for
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 3g-6
(cf. parts
(i_{2})
and
(ii)
of Proposition
3.19
), where proving that such bundles admit a presentation as in case
(2\text{-}b)
of Lemma
3.2
becomes more difficult (note, for instance, that the proof of Claim
3.20
crucially relies on the assumption
d\geq 3g-5
).

\boxed{2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu:\;\mbox{Components from modifications of Case (2-b)}}
Proposition
3.19
below will provide
parametric constructions
of components of
B^{k_{3}}d\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
arising via suitable
modifications
of presentations of bundles of type
(2\text{-}b)
in Lemma
3.2
. Namely, constructed components will be of the form
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{sup},3,(2\text{-}b)\text{-mod}}
or
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{reg},3,(2\text{-}b)\text{-mod}}
in the sense of Definition
3.3
.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. One has the following situation:

(i)
If either
(i_{1})

g\geq 6
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{2}
,
3g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
and
\nu+1\leq s\leq g-\nu+1
or
(i_{2})

g\geq 8
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+4}{4}
,
3g-3-\nu\leq d\leq 3g-6
and
g-\nu+2\leq s\leq g-1
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and superabundant, being of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is obtained by natural modification of bundles of second type as in case
(2-b)
of Lemma
3.2
. Indeed, such a bundle
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to\omega_{C}(-D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and where
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
is general.

(ii)
If either
(ii_{1})

g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+3}{4}
and
2g-7+3\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu
or
(ii_{2})

g\geq 10
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}
and
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0\,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
whereas
N\in Pic^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
is general of its degree, so non-effective and special.

Moreover, the component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, if
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu
, whereas it is regular, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, when
d=2g-7+2\nu
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-2 vector bundle of degree
d
as in Lemma
3.2
, Case (2-b); it is defined by:

(3.18)

0\to K_{C}-(A+B_{b})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-D_{s}\to 0\,,

where
A
is the unique line bundle of the gonality
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
,
D_{s}
is a general effective divisor of degree
s
(
h^{0}(D_{s})=1
), and
B_{b}
is an effective divisor of degree
b=4g-4-\nu-s-d\geq 0
. This
B_{b}
represents the base locus of the linear pencil
|A+B_{b}|
.

For
\mathcal{F}
to be a bundle of the
second type
, the kernel line bundle
N
must satisfy the condition:

(3.19)

\omega_{C}\otimes N^{\vee}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}{1,1}}}\subseteq W^{1}_{\nu+b}(C)

This ensures the bundle corresponds to the specific locus described in Lemma
2.3
-(i).

Applying the necessary condition for stability, we derive the following numerical constraints:

(3.20)

\nu+b>s\implies b>s-\nu\geq 1

Since
s-\nu\geq 1
by assumption, it follows that
B_{b}
is non-zero (
B_{b}\neq 0
). Thus, the existence of a non-trivial base locus is a fundamental requirement for the stability of
\mathcal{F}
in this configuration.

Claim 3.20
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a bundle of degree
d\geq 3g-5
and of type
(2-b)
as in Lemma
3.2
, i.e. fitting in (
3.18
), then
\mathcal{F}
fits also in:

(3.21)

0\ \to\ K_{C}-(B_{b}+D_{s})\ \to\ \mathcal{F}\ \to\ K_{C}-A\ \to\ 0.

Proof of Claim
3.20
.

Since
\mathcal{F}
fits in (
3.18
), we have
d=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})=4g-4-\nu-b-s
and, by Lemma
3.4
-(i),
\mathcal{F}
fits also in

(3.22)

0\to K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}\to 0\,.

Let
\Gamma
be a canonical section of
F=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
, corresponding to
\mathcal{F}\to\!\!\!\!\!\to\omega_{C}
. Then
\operatorname{dim}(|\Gamma|)=2
by (
1.8
) and by
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{v}-(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s})))=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v})=3
. Similarly as in the proof of Proposition
3.5
,
for
q\in F
general, the dimension of the family of unisecants in
|\Gamma|
passing through
q\in F
is
1
so
|\Gamma|
certainly contains reducible unisecants. The section contained in any such a reducible unisecant corresponds to a quotient line bundle of
\mathcal{F}
of the form
K_{C}-E_{f}
, where
E_{f}
an effective divisor containing the point
p=\rho(q)\in C
projection of
q
onto the base curve
C
.
For determinantal reasons,
\mathcal{F}
fits also in:

0\to K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f})\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-E_{f}\to 0.

From the assumption
3g-5\leq d=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})=4g-4-\nu-b-s
, it follows that
s=\mbox{deg}(D_{s})\leq g+1-\nu-b
. Thus, since
D_{s}
is general of its degree and since
h^{0}(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}))=h^{1}(A+B_{b})=g+1-\nu-b
, by Serre duality one has
h^{1}(A+B_{b}+D_{s})=h^{0}(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}))=g+1-\nu-b-s
so, by Riemann-Roch,

h^{0}(A+B_{b}+D_{s})=2
. Since
p\in C
is general such that
p\in E_{f}
, we have therefore
h^{0}(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f})\leq 1
.

Assume by contradiction that
h^{0}(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f})=0
; in such a case the coboundary map
H^{0}(K_{C}-E_{f})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f}))
arising from the above exact sequence would be surjective, so its dual map

H^{1}(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f}))^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{\vee}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(K_{C}-E_{f})^{\vee}
would be injective. Since

H^{1}(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f}))^{\vee}\simeq H^{0}(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f})\simeq{\rm Hom}(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}),K_{C}-E_{f})

and

H^{0}(K_{C}-E_{f})^{\vee}\simeq H^{1}(E_{f})\simeq{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}),\,K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f})),

we would therefore deduce that
\mathcal{F}
does not admit
K_{C}-(A+B_{b}+D_{s})
as a sub-line bundle, namely that
\mathcal{F}
would not fit in (
3.22
)
contradicting Lemma
3.4
.

Thus,
h^{0}(A+B_{b}+D_{s}-E_{f})=1
and
A-E_{f}
is effective. By the way, since
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
,
h^{1}(K_{C}-E_{f})\geq 2
,
hence
f:=\mbox{deg}(E_{f})\geq\nu
which forces
\mathcal{O}_{C}(E_{f})=A
. Thus
\mathcal{F}
fits in (
3.21
), as stated.
∎

Following Claim
3.20
, when
d\geq 3g-5
, any type (2-b) bundle
\mathcal{F}
can be realized as an extension
\mathcal{F}_{u}\in\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,\,K_{C}-(B_{b}+D_{s}))
. While stability for Case (2-b) requires a non-trivial base locus
B_{b}\neq 0
— which complicates the construction — the results of Claim
3.20
suggest a shift in strategy. Specifically, we will show that presentations of the form (
3.21
) with
b=0
generate the desired components of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
.

Claim 3.21
.

Let
g\geq 5
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{2}
,
3g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
and
\nu+1\leq s\leq g-\nu+1
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve. Then there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and superabundant, being of dimension

(3.23)

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, fitting in an exact sequence of the form

0\to\omega_{C}(-D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0\,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and where
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
is general, where as above
\nu+1\leq s\leq g-\nu+1
.

Proof of Claim
3.21
.

Set
L:=\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}
and
N:=\omega_{C}(-D_{s})
, with
s
any integer as in the statement. Therefore

d=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})=4g-4-s-\nu
and
m:=\operatorname{dim}({\rm Ext}^{1}(L,N))=h^{1}(N-L)=h^{0}(K_{C}-A+D_{s})=2g-2-\nu+s-g+1=g-1+s-\nu
, since
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A+D_{s})=2g-2+s-\nu>2g-2
. Moreover
\ell:=h^{0}(L)=2g-2-\nu-g+3=g+1-\nu>1=h^{1}(K_{C}-D_{s}):=r
. We see further that
m=g-1+s-\nu\geq\ell+1=h^{0}(L)+1=g+2-\nu
iff
s\geq 3
. Thus, from Theorem
2.2
,
\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset{\rm Ext}^{1}(L,N)
, defined as in (
2.2
), is irreducible and of
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{1})=g-1+s-\nu-(g+1-\nu)=s-2
. We also remark that, for
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
general, the corresponding rank-
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
has speciality
3
. Posing

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
, we have therefore

\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=4g-7-d-\nu=s-3
, the latter equality by definition of
d
. More precisely, since
r=h^{1}(K_{C}-D_{s})=1
, it turns out that for
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,K_{C}-D_{s})
, the associated coboundary map
H^{0}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{u}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(N)
must be the zero-map. As in the proof of Lemma
3.9
, recall that
\partial_{u}
is induced by the natural cup-product
\cup:H^{0}(L)\otimes H^{1}(N-L)\to H^{1}(N)
; this means that
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is simply

(3.24)

{\small{\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=\left(\mathrm{coker}\left(H^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A)\leftarrow H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes H^{0}(D_{s})\right)\right)^{\vee},}

i.e.
\mathcal{W}_{1}
turns out to be a sub-vector space of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,K_{C}-D_{s})
hence
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is a linear space of
\mathbb{P}:=\mathbb{P}({\rm Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee},\;\omega_{C}(-D_{s})))
. Since
D_{s}
varies in a suitable open, dense subset
S\subseteq C^{(s)}
, whereas
L=\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}
is uniquely determined, and since each corresponding linear space
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is of (constant) dimension
4g-7-d-\nu
, we get an irreducible scheme, denoted by
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
, which is ruled over
S
by the linear spaces
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
’s as
D_{s}
varies. Thus
\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot})=\operatorname{dim}(C^{(s)})+\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=2s-3=8g-11-2d-2\nu
, where the latter equality follows from
d=4g-4-s-\nu
. Similarly, we get
\rho^{k_{3}}_{d}=10g-3d-18=-2g-6+3s+3\nu
so from assumptions on
d
we notice that

(3.25)

\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot})-\rho^{k_{3}}_{d}=(2s-3)-(-2g-6+3s+3\nu)=2g+3-s-3\nu=d-2g-2\nu+7>0.

For a general
[u]\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be the associated bundle.

If
s\geq\nu+2
, we may apply the Segre invariant properties as in Proposition
1.11
. We let
\sigma=1({\rm or}\;2)\equiv 2\delta-d=s-\nu{\mbox{ (mod 2) }}
; then
\sigma\leq 2\delta-d
because
2\delta-d\geq 2
; moreover
\sigma\geq 4+d-2\delta=4+\nu-s
since
\nu+2\leq s
. Therefore,

\sigma=1
means that
1\geq 4+\nu-s
, hence
s\geq\nu+3
(being
s
and
\nu
with different parity); if otherwise
\sigma=2
, this means that
2\geq 4+\nu-s
, hence
s\geq\nu+2
which is in our assumptions. If we set
X:=\varphi_{|K_{C}+L-N|}(C)
we can apply Proposition
1.11
: if
\sigma=1
, then
2\delta-d=s-\nu
is odd hence
\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(s-\nu+1-2)}(X)=\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(s-\nu-1)}(X)
so

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(s-\nu-1)}(X)\right)=s-\nu-2<\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=s-3;

if otherwise
\sigma=2
, then
2\delta-d=s-\nu
is even hence
\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(s-\nu+2-2)}(X)=\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(s-\nu)}(X)
so

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(s-\nu)}(X)\right)=s-\nu-1<\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=s-3.

If otherwise
d
is odd and
2\delta-d=1
, any destabilizing subbundle
\mathcal{M}
would force the extension to split, contradicting the choice of a non-zero
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
.

Thus
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is endowed with a natural (rational) modular map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{d,\nu}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}&U^{s}_{C}(d)\\
(s,[u])&\longrightarrow&[\mathcal{F}_{u}]\end{array}

for which
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
. Since
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is ruled over
S
, it then follows that
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
is
uniruled
.

To show that the modular map
\pi_{d,\nu}:\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}\dashrightarrow U^{s}_{C}(d)
is birational onto its image, we analyze its fibers. Assume there exists an isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{F}_{u}
for
(s^{\prime},[u^{\prime}])\neq(s,[u])
. Determinantal constraints force
D_{s^{\prime}}=D_{s}
, meaning both extensions lie in the same projectivized space
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
. The isomorphism
\varphi
induces a commutative diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}0\to&K_{C}-D_{s}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}&\to&K_{C}-A&\to 0\\
&&&\downarrow^{\varphi}&&&\\
0\to&K_{C}-D_{s}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{F}_{u}&\to&K_{C}-A&\to 0.\end{array}

The maps
\varphi\circ\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
define global sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes(K_{C}-D_{s})^{\vee})
. Since
h^{0}(D_{s}-A)=0
, the section
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
corresponding to the quotient
K_{C}-A
is algebraically isolated, which implies
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes(K_{C}-D_{s})^{\vee})=1
. Consequently,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
must be linearly dependent. This dependency forces
[u]=[u^{\prime}]
, proving that the general fiber is a singleton and
\pi_{d,\nu}
is birational.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\text{sup, 3, (2-b)-mod}}
be the closure of
\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
. By the birationality of
\pi_{d,\nu}
and the irreducibility of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
, this locus is an irreducible, uniruled, and superabundant scheme of dimension:

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\text{sup, 3, (2-b)-mod}})=8g-11-2d-2\nu.

To show this is a generically smooth component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, it suffices to prove that the dimension of the tangent space at a general
[\mathcal{F}]
matches the dimension of the locus. This is equivalent to showing that the kernel of the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
has dimension
d-2g+7-2\nu
.

Since the coboundary map
\partial_{u}
vanishes for
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
, we have:

H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\simeq H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\oplus H^{0}(K_{C}-D_{s})\quad\text{and}\quad H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\simeq H^{0}(A)\oplus H^{0}(D_{s}).

The Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
deforms continuously from the map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
of the splitting bundle
\mathcal{F}_{0}=(K_{C}-D_{s})\oplus(K_{C}-A)
. Decomposing
\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
into line bundle multiplication maps:

\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}=\mu_{D_{s}}\oplus\mu_{K_{C}-D_{s},A}\oplus\mu_{K_{C}-A,D_{s}}\oplus\mu_{A}.

As
h^{0}(D_{s})=1
, the maps
\mu_{D_{s}}
and
\mu_{K_{C}-A,D_{s}}
are injective. Using the base-point-free pencil trick on the remaining components:

\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{0}})\simeq\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{K_{C}-D_{s},A})\oplus\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{A})\simeq H^{0}(K_{C}-A-D_{s})\oplus H^{0}(K_{C}-2A).

Substituting the dimensions
h^{0}(K_{C}-A-D_{s})=g+1-\nu-s
and
h^{0}(K_{C}-2A)=g+2-2\nu
:

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}))=2g+3-3\nu-s=d-2g+7-2\nu.

This confirms generic smoothness, which completes the proof of Claim
3.21
.
∎

The above arguments complete the proof of Case
(i_{1})
.

Concerning Case
(i_{2})
, the strategy from Claim
3.20
to obtain presentation (
3.21
) fails because the assumption
h^{0}(A+B_{b}+D_{s})=2
no longer holds under the range
g-\nu+2\leq s\leq g-1
. However, inspired by Claim
3.21
, we consider bundles
\mathcal{F}_{u}
defined by extensions:

(3.26)

0\to K_{C}-D_{s}\to\mathcal{F}u\to K_{C}-A\to 0,

where
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
and
D_{s}
is general. Although these bundles are neither of first nor of second type, the construction of the irreducible, uniruled component
\mathcal{B}{\mathrm{sup},3,(2\text{-}b)\text{-mod}}
and its dimension estimate proceed exactly as in
(i_{1})
. The only specific requirement for
(i_{2})
is verifying that the Petri map kernel maintains the dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2g+7-2\nu
, for
g-\nu+2\leq s\leq g-1
.

As in
(i_{1})
, the vanishing of the coboundary map
\partial_{u}
implies
H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\simeq H^{0}(A)\oplus H^{0}(D_{s})
. This yields a diagram of multiplication maps where the injectivity of
\mu_{D_{s}}
(since
h^{0}(D_{s})=1
) leads to:

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F},A}))=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee}).

To compute
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})
, we analyze the long exact sequence associated with the extension (
3.26
) tensored by
A
and
A^{\vee}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{v}:=\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee}
. Tensoring by
A^{\vee}
gives:

(3.27)

0\to K_{C}-D_{s}-A\to\mathcal{F}_{v}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0.

From the speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
and
h^{0}(A)=2
, we deduce
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\geq 5
. Since
h^{1}(K_{C}-2A)=3
, the extension class
v
must lie in a degeneracy locus
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}\subset\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,K_{C}-D_{s}-A)
. This
v
corresponds to a hyperplane containing the image of the restricted multiplication map:

\mu_{2}:H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{2}\to H^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A),

where
V_{2}\subset H^{0}(A+D_{s})
is a 2-dimensional subspace. By applying Riemann-Roch to
\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee}
, the computed dimensions satisfy:

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=\chi(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})+h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=d-2g+7-2\nu.

This confirms the generic smoothness of the component for the extended range of
s
, completing the proof of part (
i
).

(
ii
) We consider vector bundles
\mathcal{F}_{u}
defined by extensions

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-A\to 0,

where
N\in\text{Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
is general and
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
. Although these bundles are neither of first nor second type, the construction of the component
\mathcal{B}_{3,(2-b)\text{-mod}}
, its dimension estimate, and the proof of uniruledness proceed similarly as in cases
(i_{1})
and
(i_{2})
above.

Set
L:=K_{C}-A
; from the bounds on
\nu
, we have
\mbox{deg}(2K_{C}-N-A)>2g-2
, hence

m:=\operatorname{dim}\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N)=h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)=5g-5-d-2\nu
,
\ell:=h^{0}(L)=g+1-\nu
and
r:=h^{1}(N)=3g-3-\nu-d
. Therefore conditions of Theorem
2.2
are satisfied:
\ell>r
and
m\geq\ell+1
follow from the bounds
2g-4\leq d\leq 4g-7-\nu
. Consequently,
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is irreducible of dimension
m-c(\ell,r,1)
, where
c(\ell,r,1)=\ell-r+1=d-2g+5
. Thus,
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{W}_{1}=7g-10-2d-2\nu
. As in the proof of Claim
3.21
, by varying
N
over an open dense subset
S\subseteq\text{Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
, we obtain an irreducible scheme
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
dominating
S
with fibers
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. Its dimension is
\operatorname{dim}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}\right)=g+(7g-11-2d-2\nu)=8g-11-2d-2\nu
. Comparing this with the expected dimension
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-3d-18
, we obtain:

(3.28)

\operatorname{dim}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}\right)-\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=d-2g-2\nu+7\geq 0,

with equality holding iff
d=2g-7+2\nu
.

To prove that the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
arising from a general pair
(N,[u])\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is stable we observe that, since
d\leq 3g-4-\nu\leq 3g-6
,
setting
\delta:=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A)=2g-2-\nu
we have
2\delta-d=4g-4-2\nu-d\geq g+2-2\nu\geq 2
so, as in Proposition
1.11
, we may take
\sigma:=1\;({\rm or}\;2)\equiv 2\delta-d{\mbox{ (mod 2) }}
, which are compatible with
\sigma\leq 2\delta-d
(because
2\delta-d\geq 2
) and with
\sigma\geq 4+d-2\delta=8+2\nu+d-4g
. Applying Proposition
1.11
to
X:=\varphi_{|K_{C}+L-N|}(C)
we have that when
\sigma=1
, i.e.
d
odd, one has

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d-1)}(X)\right)=(4g-4-2\nu-d-1)-1<\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=-2d+7g-2\nu-11

iff
d<3g-5
, which certainly holds in our bounds on
d
, when otherwise
\sigma=2
, i.e.
d
even,

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d)}(X)\right)=(4g-4-2\nu-d)-1<\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=-2d+7g-2\nu-11

iff
d<3g-6
which is compatible with the bounds on
d
. This shows that the general bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable.

The above arguments ensure that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is endowed with a natural (rational) modular map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{d,\nu}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}&U^{s}_{C}(d)\\
(N,[u])&\longrightarrow&[\mathcal{F}_{u}]\end{array}

for which
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
.

Claim 3.22
.

In the above set-up,
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
is uniruled and such that

(3.29)

\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu}))=8g-11-2d-2\nu.

Proof of Claim
3.22
.

Since
\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
is dominated by
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
, it suffices to show that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is uniruled over a dense open
S\subseteq\text{Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
. This follows if each fiber
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\subset\mathbb{P}(\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N))
is unirational for
N\in S
. Recall that the coboundary maps defining
\mathcal{W}_{1}
are given by the cup-product
\cup:H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes H^{1}(N-K_{C}+A)\to H^{1}(N)
. By Serre duality, this is equivalent to the multiplication map:

\mu:H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes H^{0}(K_{C}-N)\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-A-N).

For any
1
-dimensional subspace
V_{1}\subset H^{0}(K_{C}-N)
, the restriction
\mu|_{V_{1}}:H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes V_{1}\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)
is injective via the sequence
0\to K_{C}-A\to 2K_{C}-A-N
induced by
V_{1}
. Since
m>\ell+1
, the image
\text{im}(\mu|_{V_{1}})
has dimension
\ell<m-1
and therefore is contained in a hyperplane of
H^{0}(2K_{C}-A-N)
. Let
\Lambda\cong\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(K_{C}-N))
be the variety of such subspaces
V_{1}^{\lambda}
, and let
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(2K_{C}-A-N))
. We define the incidence variety:

\mathfrak{I}:=\{(\lambda,\pi)\in\Lambda\times\mathbb{P}\mid\mu(\lambda\otimes V_{1}^{\lambda})\subseteq\pi\}.

The projection
pr_{1}:\mathfrak{I}\to\Lambda
is surjective with fibers
pr_{1}^{-1}(\lambda)\cong\mathbb{P}^{m-1-\ell}
. Thus,
\mathfrak{I}
is a projective bundle over the rational variety
\Lambda
, making
\mathfrak{I}
rational. Since
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\overline{pr_{2}(\mathfrak{I})}
, it is unirational. Consequently,
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is uniruled over
S
, and so is
\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
.

To establish (
3.29
), the birationality of
\pi_{d,\nu}
onto its image follows the same strategy as in the proof of Claim
3.15
, yielding
\operatorname{dim}(\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu}))=\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot})=8g-11-2d-2\nu
.∎

Claim 3.23
.

With above assumptions, let
N\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
be general and let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be such that it corresponds to a general element
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
. Let
F_{u}
denote the surface scroll associated to
\mathcal{F}_{u}
and let
\Gamma\subset F_{u}

be the section corresponding to the quotient line bundle
K_{C}-A
as in

(3.30)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-A\to 0.

Then
\Gamma
is linearly isolated (li) on
F_{u}
in the sense of Definition
1.3
, i.e.
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})=1
.

Proof of Claim
3.23
.

The two assertions are equivalent by (
1.8
); we thus show
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})=1
. Tensoring (
3.30
) by
A
yields:

(3.31)

0\to N+A\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A\to K_{C}\to 0.

The extension space
\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C},N+A)
is isomorphic to
\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)\cong H^{1}(N+A-K_{C})
. Let

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=\{w\in\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C},N+A)\mid\text{corank}(\partial_{w})\geq 1\}.

By Theorem
2.2
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is irreducible with expected codimension
\widetilde{c}(1)=h^{0}(K_{C})-h^{1}(N+A)+1
. A direct calculation shows that
\text{codim}(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=\widetilde{c}(1)>c(1)=\text{codim}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
. Consequently,
\operatorname{dim}(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1})<\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
.

For
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
general,
\mathcal{F}_{v}:=\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A
corresponds to an element
v\notin\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
. Thus, the coboundary map
\partial_{v}:H^{0}(K_{C})\to H^{1}(N+A)
is surjective, implying
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=h^{1}(K_{C})=1
. Since
\text{rank}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=2
,
\det(\mathcal{F}_{u})=K_{C}+N-A
, Serre duality gives
1=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=h^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee}).

∎

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
denote the closure of
\text{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
in
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. By the previous construction and the birationality of
\pi_{d,\nu}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
is a uniruled variety of dimension
8g-11-2d-2\nu
. It follows from (
3.28
) that
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
for
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
, and
\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}=\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
iff
d=2g-7+2\nu
. Consequently, for
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
, any irreducible component
\mathcal{B}
containing
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
is necessarily superabundant.

To establish that
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
is itself a component, we must show that for a general
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
, the dimension of the Zariski tangent space satisfies:

\operatorname{dim}\left(T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(B^{k_{3}}d\cap U^{s}_{C}(d))\right)=8g-11-2d-2\nu.

This would identify
\mathcal{Z}{d,\nu}
as a generically smooth component (and a regular one when
d=2g-7+2\nu
).

Recalling Remark
1.7
, the tangent space is isomorphic to
\text{coker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})
, where
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
is the Petri map of
\mathcal{F}
. Thus, since
\mathcal{F}
is stable

\operatorname{dim}\left(T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(B^{k_{3}}d\cap U^{s}_{C}(d))\right)=\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}+\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{ker}\mu{\mathcal{F}}.

Comparing this with (
3.28
), the claim reduces to proving:

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2g+7-2\nu.

Verifying this dimension is where the treatments of cases
(ii_{1})
and
(ii_{2})
diverge. We begin by addressing case
(ii_{1})
.

Claim 3.24
.

Let
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+3}{4}
and
2g-7+3\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve. Then, for
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
general as above, one has
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
.

In particular,
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2g+7-2\nu
so
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
is an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, which will be denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(2-b)-mod}
and which is generically smooth, uniruled and superabundant.

Proof of Claim
3.24
.

A general
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
is given by
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
for
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
and
N\in\text{Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
general. By Claim
3.23
,
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)=1
. Consider the exact sequence:

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(A)\xrightarrow{\mu_{\mathcal{F},A}}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(A)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)\to 0.

With
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
,
h^{0}(A)=2
, and
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)=1
, we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\geq 5
. Tensoring (
3.30
) by
A^{\vee}
gives:

(3.32)

0\to N-A\to\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0.

The bundle
\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee}
corresponds to
v\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}=\{w\in\text{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,N-A)\mid\text{corank}(\partial_{w})\geq 2\}
so to a hyperplane in
H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)
containing the image of:

(3.33)

\mu_{2}:H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{2}\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A),

where
V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\subseteq H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)
and
V_{1}\subset H^{0}(K_{C}-N)
a
1
-dimensional subspace. We will show that
\text{coker}(\mu_{2})\cong H^{0}(A)
.

If by contradiction
\operatorname{dim}(\text{coker}(\mu_{2}))>2
, let
V_{3}
be a
3
-dimensional subspace of
\text{coker}(\mu_{2})
containing
V_{2}
. For such a general
V_{3}
, the associated linear series
|V_{3}|
is base-point-free since
|A|
is. Let
V^{\prime}_{2}\subset V_{3}
correspond to a general pencil. The restriction
\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}
is injective, and
\operatorname{dim}(\text{Im}(\mu_{V_{3}}))\geq\operatorname{dim}(\text{Im}(\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}))=2g-4\nu+4
. The dimension of the family of hyperplanes containing
\text{Im}(\mu_{V_{3}})
is:

h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)-\operatorname{dim}(\text{Im}(\mu_{V_{3}}))+\operatorname{dim}(\{V_{3}\})\leq h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)-(2g-4\nu+4)+(3g-5-d).

This is strictly less than
h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)-(g-\nu+1)
whenever
d>2g+3\nu-8
. Under this assumption,
\text{corank}(\mu_{2})=2
, which implies
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
. The surjectivity of
\mu_{\mathcal{F},A}
then implies:

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=2h^{0}(\mathcal{F})-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A).

Replacing
h^{0}(\mathcal{F})=d-2g+5
and
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)=d+2\nu-2g+3
(via Riemann-Roch), we obtain:

\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=2(d-2g+5)-(d+2\nu-2g+3)=d-2g+7-2\nu.

Thus,
[\mathcal{F}]
is a smooth point of an irreducible, generically smooth component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
. Uniruledness and superabundance follow from (
3.28
) and Claim
3.23
.
∎

The above arguments complete the proof of
(ii_{1})
.

To conclude the proof of part
(ii_{2})
, it remains to establish an analogous result to Claim
3.24
, but now in the range
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
.

Claim 3.25
.

Let
g\geq 10
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}
and
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
.
Then, for
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
general as above, one has
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
.

In particular,
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2g+7-2\nu
so
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
is an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, which will be denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)}
and which is moreover generically smooth, uniruled and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod})=8g-11-2d-2\nu\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}.

Furthermore, such a component is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(2-b)-mod}
, when
d
is such that
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
, whereas it is regular, i.e.

\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,(2-b)-mod}
, if
d=2g-7+2\nu
.

Proof of Claim
3.25
.

As in the previous case, we will show
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
for a general
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu}
.

Setting
\mathcal{E}:=\omega_{C}\otimes A\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}
, Serre duality implies
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{E})
. From (
), we have
h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})=3
and
h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))=1
. The multiplication map

(3.34)

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))\to H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{E})

has rank
h^{0}(A)h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})-h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))=2(3)-1=5
. Thus, proving
h^{0}(\mathcal{E})=5
reduces to showing
\mu
is surjective.

Consider the following commutative diagram of multiplication maps:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccc}&&0&&0&&0&&0&&\\[3.44444pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[3.44444pt]
0&\rightarrow&H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C})&\rightarrow&H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C}-N-A)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{coker}({\zeta_{1}})&\rightarrow&0\\[3.44444pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[3.44444pt]
0&\rightarrow&H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(A)&\rightarrow&H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(K_{C}-N)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{coker}({\zeta_{2}})&\rightarrow&0\\[3.44444pt]
&&\;\;\;\;\;\;\downarrow^{\mu_{A,A}}&&\;\;\downarrow^{\mu}&&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\downarrow^{\mu_{A,K_{C}-N}}&&\downarrow^{\mu_{\mathrm{coker}}}&&\\[3.44444pt]
0&\rightarrow&H^{0}(2A)&\rightarrow&H^{0}(\mathcal{E})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta_{3}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C}+A-N)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{coker}({\zeta_{3}})&\rightarrow&0\end{array}

Since
h^{0}(2A)=3
and
\operatorname{dim}\operatorname{ker}\mu_{A,A}=1
, then
\mu_{A,A}
is surjective. By the assumptions on
d
and
\nu
,
K_{C}-N-A
and its tensor products by
A
and
2A
are non-special. Consequently,
\text{rk}(\mu_{A,K_{C}-N})=2h^{0}(K_{C}-N)-h^{0}(K_{C}-N-A)=h^{0}(K_{C}-N+A)
, making
\mu_{A,K_{C}-N}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{coker}}
surjective. A dimension count on the cokernels yields
\text{rk}(\zeta_{3})=2
, which confirms
h^{0}(\mathcal{E})=5
.

It follows that
\mu_{\mathcal{F},A}
is surjective; thus, using
h^{0}(\mathcal{F})=d-2g+5
and
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)=d+2\nu-2g+3
, one can compute:

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=2(d-2g+5)-(d+2\nu-2g+3)=d-2g+7-2\nu.

This identifies
[\mathcal{F}]
as a smooth point of a generically smooth irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
which, by construction, is uniruled moreover superabundant for
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
whereas regular for
d=2g-7+2\nu
.
∎

The above arguments conclude also the proof of
(ii_{2})
and so of the Proposition.
∎

3.6.
First-type superabundant loci in components from second-type modifications

Regarding potential components consisting of first-type bundles from Case
(1-b)
of Lemma
3.2
, recall that Propositions
3.8
-
(i)
and
3.12
-(1) show these do not form irreducible components of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
in the ranges:
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
\frac{10}{3}g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
;
3\leq\nu\leq d\leq\frac{g}{2}
and
3g-6-\nu\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
. For smaller values of
d
, we have the following preliminary result:

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
with
\nu\geq 3
. If
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is an irreducible locus such that

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B})\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
and whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
is assumed to correspond to a rank-
2
, degree
d
stable bundle
\mathcal{F}
of speciality
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
as in case
(1-b)
of Proposition
3.12
-
(1-b)
, then necessarily one has

(3.35)

3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-4}{3}\ {\mbox{ and }}\ \frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu.

Furthermore, for any
d
as in (
3.35
) and for any integer

\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g-2\nu-1
, there actually exist such irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}:=\mathcal{B}\subset B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
presented as a general extension of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-(A+M)\to 0,

where
A
the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
,
M
an effective divisor of movable points on
C
of degree
n:=\mbox{deg}(M)
, for some
\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g-2\nu-1
as above, and such that
A+M\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{\nu+n}(C)
is general in the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
as in Lemma
2.4
, i.e.
|A+M|=g^{2}_{\nu+n}
is base-point-free and birationally very ample, and where furthermore
N
is general of its degree, such that
g+\nu-1\leq\mbox{deg}(N)\leq g+n-5
.

Proof.

We consider case
(1\text{-}b)
from Proposition
3.12
, with
2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu
. We construct irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{3,n}\subset B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}_{u}]
is defined by:

(u):\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-(A+M)\to 0,

where
\delta:=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-(A+M))
,
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
, and
M
is an effective divisor of degree
n
such that
|A+M|
is a base-point-free
g^{2}_{\nu+n}
on
C
.

We take
A+M
general in the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{\nu+n}(C)
. Setting
n=\mbox{deg}(M)=2g-2-\delta-\nu
, the condition
n\geq\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}
follows from Lemma
3.2
-(1-b), which requires
\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}
for the non-emptiness and expected dimension of the locus
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
. Furthermore,
h^{1}(K_{C}-A-M)=h^{0}(A+M)=3
, so
h^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)=\delta-g+4=g+2-\nu-n
. For this to be effective, we must have
n\leq g+1-\nu
. Summarizing the bounds:

\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g+1-\nu\;\;{\rm and}\;\;g\leq\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}.

Under such numerical constraints, any locus of dimension at least
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
necessarily satisfies (
3.35
).

To explicitly construct these stable bundles, we proceed as follows:

Claim 3.27
.

In the above assumptions for
d
,
n
,
\nu
and
C
, let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be a rank-
2
vector bundle arising as a general extension
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),N)
, where
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)
is general. Then
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable and such that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=h^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M))=3
.

Proof of Claim
3.27
.

Set
L:=K_{C}-(A+M)
, with
\mbox{deg}(L)=\delta
. Let
N\in\text{Pic}^{d-\delta}(C)
be general. The stability condition
2\delta-d>0
implies
\mbox{deg}(N)<\mbox{deg}(L)
, so
N\not\cong L
. Thus, by (
1.16
),
m:=\operatorname{dim}(\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=2\delta-d+g-1
. Let
\ell:=h^{0}(L)=\delta-g+4=g+2-\nu-n
. Under the stated bounds on
\nu
and
n
, the condition
m\geq\ell+1
from Theorem
2.2
is satisfied since
2\delta-d\geq 4-\nu-n
. Let
r:=h^{1}(N)
; if
\mbox{deg}(N)<g-1
, then
h^{0}(N)=0
and
r=3g-3-\nu-n-d>0
. In this case,
\ell\geq r
holds because
d\geq 2g-2
. If otherwise
\mbox{deg}(N)\geq g-1
, then
r=0
and
\ell\geq r
holds trivially. In both cases, Theorem
2.2
ensures the surjectivity of the coboundary map
\partial_{u}
for a general
u\in\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
, hence
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
.

Concerning the stability of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
, we distinguish two cases. If
2\delta-d\geq 2
, we may apply Proposition
1.11
to
X\subset\mathbb{P}(\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N))
being the image of
C
via the morphism associated to
|K_{C}+L-N|
. Since
\operatorname{dim}\text{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d+\sigma-2)}(X)<m-1=\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N)))
for
\sigma\leq g
, general
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is certainly stable.

If
2\delta-d=1
, hence
d
is odd,
\delta=(d+1)/2
, and
m=g
; in such a case, if
\mathcal{F}_{u}
were unstable, there would exist a sub-line bundle
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{F}_{u}
with
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})\geq(d+1)/2
. Since
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})>\mbox{deg}(N)
, the composition
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to L
is non-zero. As
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})\geq\mbox{deg}(L)
, this map would be an isomorphism, implying the sequence giving rise to
\mathcal{F}_{u}
being spliting. This contradicts the generality of
u\in\text{Ext}^{1}(L,N)\cong H^{1}(N-L)
.
Thus,
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable also in this case, completing the proof of the Claim.
∎

From the above arguments, for any
n
and
\nu
as in the statement of the Lemma, one can consider a vector bundle
\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n}
on a suitable open, dense subset
S\subseteq\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)\times\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)\times W^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}(C)
, filled-up by pairs
(N,A(M))
varying, whose rank is

m=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-M,\,N))=2\delta-d+g-1=5g-5-d-2\nu-2n
as in (
1.16
). Taking the associated projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})\to S
(consisting of family of
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),N)\right)
’s as
(N,\,A+M)
varies in
S
) and since
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}})=3g-6-2\delta-\nu=2n+\nu-g-2
by Lemma
2.4
, one has

\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n}))=g+(5g-6-d-2\nu-2n)+(2n+\nu-g-2)=5g-8-d-\nu,

the latter being bigger than
2g-2
since, in any case, from above we must have
d\leq 3g-7-\nu
. From Claim
3.27
, for any
d
,
\nu
and
n
as in Lemma
3.26
, one has therefore a natural (rational) modular map

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{d,\nu,n}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}

\displaystyle U_{C}(d)

\displaystyle(N,A+M,[u])\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{[}F_{u}],

which gives
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu,n}:=\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n})\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. If in particular
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}})=3g-6-2\delta-\nu=2n+\nu-g-2=0
, then
\operatorname{dim}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})

has several components.

Claim 3.28
.

For any integers
d
,
\nu
and
n
as in Lemma
3.26
and for
C
a general
\nu
-gonal curve, the map
\pi_{d,\nu,n}
is generically finite onto its image. In particular,
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu,n}
is uniruled and
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu,n})=\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n}))=5g-8-d-\nu
.

Proof of Claim
3.28
.

The uniruledness of
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu,n}
follows from the fact that it is dominated by the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})
over the base
S
. To determine its dimension, we prove that the modular map
\pi_{d,\nu,n}
is generically finite.

Let
[\mathcal{F}]=[\mathcal{F}_{u}]
be a general point in
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu,n}
. We examine the fiber:

\pi^{-1}([\mathcal{F}_{u}])=\{(N^{\prime},A+M^{\prime},[u^{\prime}])\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})\mid\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{F}_{u}\}.

If
A+M^{\prime}\cong A+M
, then
N^{\prime}\cong N
because the determinants are isomorphic. If
[u^{\prime}]\neq[u]
, the stability of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
implies any isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{F}_{u}
yields two sections
s_{1},s_{2}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})
. Since
\mbox{deg}(L-N)=2\delta-d\leq g-1
and
N
is general,
h^{0}(L-N)=0
. By (
1.6
), the section
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
is algebraically isolated, implying
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})=1
. Thus,
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
must be linearly dependent, which leads to the contradiction
[u^{\prime}]=[u]
as in Claim
3.15
.

If otherwise
A+M^{\prime}\not\cong A+M
, let
L^{\prime}:=K_{C}-(A+M^{\prime})
. The determinant condition requires
N^{\prime}\cong N+M^{\prime}-M
. We have therefore two distinct saturated sub-line bundles
N
and
N^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
. By the generality of
N
, the degrees
\mbox{deg}(L-N)
and
\mbox{deg}(L^{\prime}-N^{\prime})
are such that the corresponding sections in the ruled surface
F_{u}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
are algebraically isolated. Consequently, the set of such sub-line bundles, denoted by
\text{Div}^{1,\delta}_{F_{u}}
, consists of only finitely many reduced points.

In both cases, the fiber
\pi^{-1}([\mathcal{F}_{u}])
is finite, proving that
\pi_{d,\nu,n}
is generically finite onto its image.
∎

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
denote the closure in
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
of
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu,n}
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
is
uniruled
of dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n})=5g-8-d-\nu
, as
\mathcal{Z}_{d,\nu,n}
is dense in it. Notice first that, for any
\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g+1-\nu
as above, the dimension of the locus
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
does not depend on
n=\mbox{deg}(M)
; furthermore notice that

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n})=5g-8-d-\nu\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d\;\;\Leftrightarrow\;\;d\geq\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2},

proving the lower-bound on
d
as in (
3.35
) (recall that the upper-bound is given by what proved for case
(1-b)
in Proposition
3.8
and
3.12
). Therefore we get
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-4}{3}
in (
3.35
) which proves the Lemma.
∎

\boxed{\mbox{Further obstructions: no component from Case $(1-b)$ as}\;\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}\subsetneq\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}}
Once the irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
have been constructed as in Lemma
3.26
